# Mount Everest

Not to be confused with [Mount Everett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Everett).

Several terms redirect here. For other uses, see [Everest (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_\(disambiguation\)), [Sagarmatha (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagarmatha_\(disambiguation\)), and [Qomolangma (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qomolangma_\(disambiguation\)).

North Face of Everest as seen from the path to [North Base Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_base_camps)



View from the south (Nepal) shows Everest rising behind the ridge connecting [Nuptse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuptse) and [Lhotse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhotse); in the foreground are [Thamserku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thamserku), [Kangtega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangtega), and [Ama Dablam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ama_Dablam)

**Mount Everest** (known in [Nepali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language) as **Sagarmāthā**[^cite_note-8], in [Sherpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherpa_language) as **Chamolangma**[^cite_note-9], and in [Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_language_\(standard\)) as **Jomolangma**/**Čhomolangma**[^cite_note-10] is the highest mountain on Earth above [sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level). It lies in the [Mahalangur Himal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahalangur_Himal) sub-range of the [Himalayas](37966.md) and marks part of the [China–Nepal border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Nepal_border) at its summit.[^cite_note-11] Its height was most recently measured in 2020 through a joint survey by Nepalese and Chinese authorities as 8,848.86 m (29,031 ft 8+1⁄2 in).[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13]&#x20;

Mount Everest attracts many climbers, including highly experienced mountaineers. There are two main climbing routes, one approaching the summit from the southeast in [Nepal](171166.md) (known as the standard route) and the other from the north in [China](5405.md). While not posing substantial technical climbing challenges on the standard route, Everest presents dangers such as [altitude sickness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_sickness), weather, and wind, as well as hazards from [avalanches](73321.md) and the [Khumbu Icefall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu_Icefall). As of May 2024, 340 people have [died on Everest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_who_died_climbing_Mount_Everest). Over 200 bodies remain on the mountain and have not been removed due to the dangerous conditions.[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-Nuwer-15]&#x20;

Climbers typically ascend only part of Mount Everest's elevation, as the mountain's full elevation is measured from the [geoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoid), which approximates sea level. The closest sea to Mount Everest's summit is the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md), almost 700 km (430 mi) away. To approximate a climb of the entire height of Mount Everest, one would need to start from this coastline, a feat accomplished by [Tim Macartney-Snape's team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Macartney-Snape) in 1990. Climbers usually begin their ascent from base camps above 5,000 m (16,404 ft). The amount of elevation climbed from below these camps varies. On the Tibetan side, most climbers drive directly to the [North Base Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_base_camps). On the Nepalese side, climbers generally fly into [Kathmandu](17168.md), then [Lukla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lukla), and trek to the [South Base Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_base_camps), making the climb from Lukla to the summit about 6,000 m (20,000 ft) in elevation gain.&#x20;

The first recorded efforts to reach Everest's summit were made by British [mountaineers](20341.md). As Nepal did not allow foreigners to enter the country at the time, the British made several attempts on the North Ridge route from the Tibetan side. After the first [reconnaissance expedition by the British in 1921](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1921_British_Mount_Everest_reconnaissance_expedition) reached 7,000 m (22,966 ft) on the [North Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Col), the [1922 expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1922_British_Mount_Everest_expedition) on its first summit attempt marked the first time a human had climbed above 8,000 m (26,247 ft) and it also pushed the North Ridge route up to 8,321 m (27,300 ft). On the [1924 expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_British_Mount_Everest_expedition), [George Mallory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mallory) and [Andrew Irvine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Irvine_\(mountaineer\)) made a final summit attempt on 8 June but never returned, leading to debate as to whether they were the first to reach the top. [Tenzing Norgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzing_Norgay) and [Edmund Hillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Hillary) made the [first documented ascent of Everest in 1953](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_British_Mount_Everest_expedition), using the Southeast Ridge route. Norgay had reached 8,595 m (28,199 ft) the previous year as a member of the [1952 Swiss expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Swiss_Mount_Everest_expedition). The Chinese mountaineering team of [Wang Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Fuzhou), [Gonpo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gongbu_\(mountaineer\)), and Qu Yinhua made the first reported [ascent of the peak from the North Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_Chinese_Mount_Everest_expedition) on 25 May 1960.[^cite_note-NorthRidge-16] &#x20;

## Name



The name "Mount Everest" was first proposed in this 1856 speech, later published in 1857, in which the mountain was first confirmed as the world's highest.

Mount Everest's [Nepali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language) name is सगरमाथा = *Sagarmāthā* (in [IAST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Alphabet_of_Sanskrit_Transliteration) transcription), pronounced [\[sʌɡʌrmatʰa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Nepali), lit. ''head in the sky'' (loosely translated as "goddess of the sky"[^cite_note-17]).[^cite_note-18]&#x20;

The [Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Tibetan) name for Everest is *Qomolangma* ([ཇོ་མོ་གླང་མ](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E0%BD%87%E0%BD%BC%E0%BC%8B%E0%BD%98%E0%BD%BC%E0%BC%8B%E0%BD%82%E0%BE%B3%E0%BD%84%E0%BC%8B%E0%BD%98), lit. "holy mother"). The name was first recorded (in a Chinese transcription) in the 1721 Kangxi Atlas, issued during the reign of [Qing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_China) [Emperor Kangxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_Kangxi); it first appeared in the West in 1733 as *Tchoumour Lancma*, on a map prepared by the French geographer [D'Anville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Baptiste_Bourguignon_d%27Anville) based on the Kangxi Atlas.[^cite_note-19] The Tibetan name is also popularly romanised as *Chomolungma* and (in [Wylie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wylie_transliteration)) as *Jo-mo-glang-ma*.[^cite_note-24]&#x20;

The official [Chinese transcription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcription_into_Chinese_characters) is [珠穆朗玛峰](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E7%8F%A0%E7%A9%86%E6%9C%97%E7%8E%9B%E5%B3%B0) ([t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters) [珠穆朗瑪峰](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E7%8F%A0%E7%A9%86%E6%9C%97%E7%91%AA%E5%B3%B0)), or *Zhūmùlǎngmǎ Fēng* in [pinyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinyin). It is a phonetic transcription of the Tibetan name into [Standard Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chinese). While other Chinese names have been used historically, including *Shèngmǔ Fēng* ([t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_characters) [聖母峰](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E8%81%96%E6%AF%8D%E5%B3%B0), [s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_characters) [圣母峰](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%9C%A3%E6%AF%8D%E5%B3%B0), lit. "holy mother peak"), these names were largely phased out after the Chinese [Ministry of Internal Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Civil_Affairs) issued a decree to adopt a sole name in May 1952.[^cite_note-25]&#x20;

The British geographic survey of 1849 attempted to preserve local names when possible (e.g., [Kangchenjunga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangchenjunga) and [Dhaulagiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaulagiri)). However, [Andrew Waugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Scott_Waugh), the British [Surveyor General of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveyor_General_of_India), claimed that he could not find a commonly used local name, and that his search for one had been hampered by the Nepalese and Tibetan policy of exclusion of foreigners. Waugh argued that – because there were many local names – it would be difficult to favour one name over all others; he therefore decided that Peak XV should be named after British surveyor [Sir George Everest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Everest), his predecessor as Surveyor General of India.[^cite_note-everest_bwp70-26][^cite_note-ReferenceA-27][^cite_note-rgs1857-28] Everest himself opposed the honour, and told the [Royal Geographical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Geographical_Society) in 1857 that "Everest" could neither be written in [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi) nor pronounced by "[the native of India](7564733.md)". Despite Everest's objections, Waugh's proposed name prevailed, and the Royal Geographical Society officially adopted the name "Mount Everest" in 1865.[^cite_note-everest_bwp70-26][^cite_note-29] The modern pronunciation of Everest ([/ˈɛvərɪst/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English))[^cite_note-30] is different from Sir George's pronunciation of his surname ([/ˈiːvrɪst/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*EEV-rist*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key)).[^cite_note-31]&#x20;

In the late 19th century, many European [cartographers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartography) incorrectly believed that a native name for the mountain was [Gaurishankar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaurishankar), a mountain between [Kathmandu](17168.md) and Everest.[^cite_note-32]&#x20;

## Surveys

### 19th century



Mount Everest [relief map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartographic_relief_depiction)



Profiles of the full elevation of Mount Everest

In 1802, the British began the [Great Trigonometrical Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Trigonometrical_Survey) of India to fix, among other things, the locations, heights, and names of the world's highest mountains. Starting in southern India, the survey teams moved northward using giant [theodolites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodolite), each weighing 500 kg (1,100 lb) and requiring 12 men to carry, to measure heights as accurately as possible. They reached the Himalayan foothills by the 1830s, but [Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Nepal) was unwilling to allow the British to enter the country due to suspicions of their intentions. Several requests by the surveyors to enter Nepal were denied.[^cite_note-everest_bwp70-26]&#x20;

The British were forced to continue their observations from [Terai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terai), a region south of Nepal which is parallel to the Himalayas. Conditions in Terai were difficult because of torrential rains and [malaria](20423.md). Three survey officers died from malaria while two others had to retire because of failing health.[^cite_note-everest_bwp70-26]&#x20;

Nonetheless, in 1847, the British continued the survey and began detailed observations of the Himalayan peaks from observation stations up to 240 km (150 mi) distant. Weather restricted work to the last three months of the year. In November 1847, [Andrew Scott Waugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Scott_Waugh), the British [Surveyor General of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveyor_General_of_India), made several observations from the Sawajpore station at the east end of the Himalayas. [Kangchenjunga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangchenjunga) was then [considered the highest peak in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_past_presumed_highest_mountains), and with interest, he noted a peak beyond it, about 230 km (140 mi) away. John Armstrong, one of Waugh's subordinates, also saw the peak from a site farther west and called it peak "b". Waugh would later write that the observations indicated that peak "b" was higher than Kangchenjunga, but closer observations were required for verification. The following year, Waugh sent a survey official back to Terai to make closer observations of peak "b", but clouds thwarted his attempts.[^cite_note-everest_bwp70-26]&#x20;

In 1849, Waugh dispatched James Nicolson to the area, who made two observations from Jirol, 190 km (120 mi) away. Nicolson then took the largest [theodolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodolite) and headed east, obtaining over 30 observations from five different locations, with the closest being 174 km (108 mi) from the peak.[^cite_note-everest_bwp70-26]&#x20;

Nicolson retreated to [Patna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patna) on the [Ganges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges) to perform the necessary calculations based on his observations. His raw data gave an average height of 9,200 m (30,200 ft) for peak "b", but this did not consider [light refraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_refraction), which distorts heights. However, the number clearly indicated that peak "b" was higher than Kangchenjunga. Nicolson contracted malaria and was forced to return home without finishing his calculations. Michael Hennessy, one of Waugh's assistants, had begun designating peaks based on [Roman numerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_numerals), with Kangchenjunga named Peak IX. Peak "b" now became known as Peak XV.[^cite_note-everest_bwp70-26]&#x20;

In 1852, stationed at the survey headquarters in [Dehradun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dehradun), [Radhanath Sikdar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radhanath_Sikdar), an Indian mathematician and surveyor from [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal) was the first to identify Everest as the world's highest peak, using [trigonometric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometry) calculations based on Nicolson's measurements.[^cite_note-35] An official announcement that Peak XV was the highest was delayed for several years as the calculations were repeatedly verified. Waugh began work on Nicolson's data in 1854, and along with his staff spent almost two years working on the numbers, having to deal with the problems of light refraction, barometric pressure, and temperature over the vast distances of the observations. Finally, in March 1856 he announced his findings in a letter to his deputy in [Calcutta](47905.md). Kangchenjunga was declared to be 8,582 m (28,156 ft), while Peak XV was given the height of 8,840 m (29,002 ft). Waugh concluded that Peak XV was "most probably the highest in the world".[^cite_note-everest_bwp70-26] Peak XV (measured in feet) was calculated to be exactly 29,000 ft (8,839.2 m) high, but was publicly declared to be 29,002 ft (8,839.8 m) in order to avoid the impression that an exact height of 29000 ft was nothing more than a rounded estimate.[^cite_note-36] Waugh is sometimes playfully credited with being "the first person to put two feet on top of Mount Everest".[^cite_note-37]&#x20;

### 20th century



Published by the Survey of [Nepal](171166.md), this is Map 50 of the 57 map set at 1\:50,000 scale "attached to the main text on the First Joint Inspection Survey, 1979–80, [Nepal](171166.md)-China border." At the top centre, a boundary line, identified as separating "China" and "Nepal", passes through the summit contour. The boundary here and for much of the [China–Nepal border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Nepal_border) follows the main Himalayan watershed divide.



[Kangshung Face (the east face)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangshung_Face) as seen from orbit

In 1856, Andrew Waugh announced Everest (then known as Peak XV) as 8,840 m (29,002 ft) high, after several years of calculations based on observations made by the [Great Trigonometrical Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Trigonometrical_Survey).[^cite_note-38] From 1952 to 1954, the [Survey of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survey_of_India), using [triangulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangulation) methods, determined that the height of Everest was 8,847.73 m (29,028 ft).[^cite_note-39] In 1975 it was subsequently reaffirmed by a Chinese measurement of 8,848.13 m (29,029.30 ft).[^cite_note-ABC_au-40] In both cases the snow cap, not the rock head, was measured. The 8,848 m (29,029 ft) height given was officially recognised by Nepal and China.[^cite_note-41] Nepal planned a new survey in 2019 to determine if the [April 2015 Nepal earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2015_Nepal_earthquake) affected the height of the mountain.[^cite_note-42]&#x20;

In May 1999, an American Everest expedition directed by [Bradford Washburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradford_Washburn) anchored a [GPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Positioning_System) unit into the highest bedrock. A rock head elevation of 8,850 m (29,035 ft), and a snow/ice elevation 1 m (3 ft) higher, were obtained via this device.[^cite_note-alpres-43] [Geoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoid) uncertainty casts doubt upon the accuracy claimed by both the 1999 and 2005 (see [§ 21st-century surveys](#21st-century_surveys)) surveys.[^cite_note-44]&#x20;

In 1955, a detailed [photogrammetric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photogrammetry) map (at a scale of 1\:50,000) of the [Khumbu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu) region, including the south side of Mount Everest, was made by [Erwin Schneider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erwin_Schneider) as part of the 1955 International Himalayan Expedition, which also attempted [Lhotse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhotse).&#x20;

In the late 1980s, an even more detailed [topographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography) map of the Everest area was made under the direction of Bradford Washburn, using extensive [aerial photography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerial_photography).[^cite_note-washburn_map-45]&#x20;

### 21st century

On 9 October 2005, after several months of measurement and calculation, the [Chinese Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Academy_of_Sciences) and State Bureau of Surveying and Mapping announced the height of Everest as 8,844.43 m (29,017.16 ft) with accuracy of ±0.21 m (8.3 in), claiming it was the most accurate and precise measurement to date.[^cite_note-46] This height is based on the highest point of rock and not the snow and ice covering it. The Chinese team measured a snow-ice depth of 3.5 m (11 ft),[^cite_note-ABC_au-40] which is in agreement with a net elevation of 8,848 m (29,029 ft). An argument arose between China and Nepal as to whether the official height should be the rock height (8,844 m, China) or the snow height (8,848 m, Nepal). In 2010, both sides agreed that the height of Everest is 8,848 m, and Nepal recognises China's claim that the rock height of Everest is 8,844 m.[^cite_note-47] On 8 December 2020, it was jointly announced by the two countries that the new official height is 8,848.86 metres (29,031.7 ft).[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49]&#x20;

It is thought that the [plate tectonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonics) of the [Main Himalayan Thrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Himalayan_Thrust) and related faults, which form the [convergent boundary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergent_boundary) between the [Eurasian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Plate) and [Indian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate), are adding to the height and moving the summit northeastwards. Two accounts suggest the rates of change are 4 mm (0.16 in) per year vertically and 3 to 6 mm (0.12 to 0.24 in) per year horizontally,[^cite_note-alpres-43][^cite_note-50] but another account mentions more lateral movement (27 mm or 1.1 in),[^cite_note-51] and even shrinkage has been suggested.[^cite_note-52]&#x20;

### Comparisons

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of highest mountains on Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_highest_mountains_on_Earth).

The summit of Everest is the point at which Earth's surface reaches the greatest distance [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_sea_level). Several other mountains are sometimes claimed to be the "tallest mountains on Earth". [Mauna Kea](267366.md) in Hawaii is tallest when measured from its base;[^cite_note-base-53] it rises over 10,200 m (33,464.6 ft) from its base on the mid-ocean floor, but only attains 4,205 m (13,796 ft) above sea level.&#x20;

By the same measure of base to summit, [Denali](207247.md) (also called Mount McKinley) in [Alaska](624.md) is taller than Everest as well.[^cite_note-base-53] Despite its height above sea level of only 6,190 m (20,308 ft), Denali sits atop a sloping plain with elevations from 300 to 900 m (980 to 2,950 ft), yielding a height above base in the range of 5,300 to 5,900 m (17,400 to 19,400 ft); a commonly quoted figure is 5,600 m (18,400 ft).[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55] By comparison, reasonable base elevations for Everest range from 4,200 m (13,800 ft) on the south side to 5,200 m (17,100 ft) on the [Tibetan Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Plateau), yielding a height above base in the range of 3,650 to 4,650 m (11,980 to 15,260 ft).[^cite_note-washburn_map-45]&#x20;

The summit of [Chimborazo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chimborazo) in [Ecuador](9334.md) is 2,168 m (7,113 ft) farther from Earth's centre (6,384.4 km or 3,967.1 mi) than that of Everest (6,382.3 km, 3,965.8 mi), because the Earth bulges at the equator.[^cite_note-56] This is despite Chimborazo having a peak of 6,268 m (20,564.3 ft) above sea level versus Mount Everest's 8,848 m (29,028.9 ft).&#x20;

### Context and maps



3D rendering of Mount Everest and surrounding terrain

Many of the highest mountains in the world are near Mount Everest, for example [Lhotse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhotse), 8,516 m (27,940 ft); [Nuptse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuptse), 7,855 m (25,771 ft), [Changtse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changtse), 7,580 m (24,870 ft) and [Khumbutse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbutse), 6,636 m (21,772 ft). On the southwest side, a major feature in the lower areas is the [Khumbu icefall and glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu_Glacier), an obstacle to climbers on those routes but also to the base camps.&#x20;



Southern and northern climbing routes as seen from the [International Space Station](15043.md) (The names on the photo are links to corresponding pages.)

## Geology

Geologists have subdivided the rocks comprising Mount Everest into three units called [formations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geologic_formation).[^cite_note-Scientiav5-57][^cite_note-IslandArcv14-58] Each formation is separated from the other by low-angle [faults](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fault_\(geology\)), called [detachments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detachment_fault), along which they have been thrust southward over each other. From the summit of Mount Everest to its base these rock units are the Qomolangma Formation, the [North Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Col) Formation, and the [Rongbuk Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rongbuk_Glacier).&#x20;

The Qomolangma Formation, also known as the Jolmo Lungama Formation,[^cite_note-Everest1953Geo-59] runs from the summit to the top of the Yellow Band, about 8,600 m (28,200 ft) above sea level. It consists of greyish to dark grey or white, parallel laminated and bedded, [Ordovician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordovician) limestone interlayered with subordinate beds of recrystallised [dolomite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolomite_\(rock\)) with [argillaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argillaceous_minerals) [laminae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sediment) and [siltstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siltstone). Gansser first reported finding microscopic fragments of [crinoids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crinoid) in this [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone).[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] Later [petrographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrograph) analysis of samples of the limestone from near the summit revealed them to be composed of carbonate pellets and finely fragmented remains of [trilobites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trilobite), crinoids, and [ostracods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostracods). Other samples were so badly sheared and recrystallised that their original constituents could not be determined. A thick, white-weathering [thrombolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrombolite) bed that is 60 m (200 ft) thick comprises the foot of the "[Third Step](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Steps)", and base of the summit pyramid of Everest. This bed, which crops out starting about 70 m (230 ft) below the summit of Mount Everest, consists of sediments trapped, bound, and cemented by the biofilms of micro-organisms, especially [cyanobacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyanobacteria), in shallow marine waters. The Qomolangma Formation is broken up by several high-angle faults that terminate at the low angle [normal fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_fault), the Qomolangma Detachment. This detachment separates it from the underlying Yellow Band. The lower five metres of the Qomolangma Formation overlying this detachment are very highly deformed.[^cite_note-Scientiav5-57][^cite_note-IslandArcv14-58][^cite_note-MyrowOthers2009-62]&#x20;

The bulk of Mount Everest, between 7,000 and 8,600 m (23,000 and 28,200 ft), consists of the [North Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Col) Formation, of which the Yellow Band forms the upper part between 8,200 to 8,600 m (26,900 to 28,200 ft). The Yellow Band consists of [intercalated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercalation_\(geology\)) beds of Middle Cambrian [diopside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diopside)-[epidote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidote)-bearing [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble), which weathers a distinctive yellowish brown, and [muscovite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscovite)-[biotite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biotite) phyllite and [semischist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schist). Petrographic analysis of marble collected from about 8,300 m (27,200 ft) found it to consist as much as five per cent of the ghosts of recrystallised crinoid ossicles. The upper five metres of the Yellow Band lying adjacent to the Qomolangma Detachment is badly deformed. A 5–40 cm (2.0–15.7 in) thick fault [breccia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breccia) separates it from the overlying Qomolangma Formation.[^cite_note-Scientiav5-57][^cite_note-IslandArcv14-58][^cite_note-MyrowOthers2009-62]&#x20;

The remainder of the North Col Formation, exposed between 7,000 to 8,200 m (23,000 to 26,900 ft) on Mount Everest, consists of interlayered and deformed schist, [phyllite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phyllite), and minor marble. Between 7,600 and 8,200 m (24,900 and 26,900 ft), the North Col Formation consists chiefly of biotite-quartz phyllite and chlorite-biotite phyllite intercalated with minor amounts of biotite-[sericite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sericite)-quartz schist. Between 7,000 and 7,600 m (23,000 and 24,900 ft), the lower part of the North Col Formation consists of biotite-quartz schist intercalated with epidote-quartz schist, biotite-calcite-quartz schist, and thin layers of [quartzose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quartz) [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble). These metamorphic rocks appear to be the result of the metamorphism of Middle to Early [Cambrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambrian) deep sea [flysch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flysch) composed of interbedded, [mudstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudstone), [shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shale), clayey [sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone), calcareous sandstone, [graywacke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graywacke), and sandy limestone. The base of the North Col Formation is a regional low-angle normal fault called the "Lhotse detachment".[^cite_note-Scientiav5-57][^cite_note-IslandArcv14-58][^cite_note-MyrowOthers2009-62]&#x20;

Below 7,000 m (23,000 ft), the Rongbuk Formation underlies the North Col Formation and forms the base of Mount Everest. It consists of [sillimanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sillimanite)-[K-feldspar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-feldspar) grade schist and [gneiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gneiss) intruded by numerous [sills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sill_\(geology\)) and [dikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dike_\(geology\)) of [leucogranite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granite) ranging in thickness from 1 cm to 1,500 m (0.4 in to 4,900 ft).[^cite_note-IslandArcv14-58][^cite_note-63] These leucogranites are part of a belt of Late [Oligocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oligocene)–[Miocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miocene) intrusive rocks known as the Higher Himalayan leucogranite. They formed as the result of partial melting of [Paleoproterozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleoproterozoic) to Ordovician high-grade metasedimentary rocks of the Higher Himalayan Sequence about 20 to 24 million years ago during the subduction of the Indian Plate.[^cite_note-64]&#x20;

Mount Everest consists of [sedimentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedimentary_rock) and [metamorphic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metamorphic_rock) rocks that have been faulted southward over continental crust composed of Archean granulites of the Indian Plate [during the Cenozoic collision of India with Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_the_Himalaya).[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] Current interpretations argue that the Qomolangma and North Col formations consist of marine sediments that accumulated within the continental shelf of the northern passive continental margin of India before it collided with Asia. The Cenozoic collision of India with Asia subsequently deformed and metamorphosed these strata as it thrust them southward and upward.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] The Rongbuk Formation consists of a sequence of high-grade metamorphic and granitic rocks that were derived from the alteration of high-grade metasedimentary rocks. During the collision of India with Asia, these rocks were thrust downward and to the north as they were overridden by other strata; heated, metamorphosed, and partially melted at depths of over 15 to 20 kilometres (9.3 to 12.4 mi) below sea level; and then forced upward to surface by thrusting towards the south between two major detachments.[^cite_note-70] Mount Everest is rising by about 2 mm per year.[^cite_note-71]&#x20;

### IUGS geological heritage site

In respect of the recognition of the "highest rocks on the planet" as fossiliferous, marine limestone, the Ordovician Rocks of Mount Everest were included by the [International Union of Geological Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Union_of_Geological_Sciences) (IUGS) in its assemblage of 100 geological heritage sites around the world in a listing published in October 2022. The organisation defines an IUGS Geological Heritage Site as "a key place with geological elements and/or processes of international scientific relevance, used as a reference, and/or with a substantial contribution to the development of geological sciences through history."[^cite_note-72]&#x20;



Mount Everest with snow melted, showing upper geologic layers in bands

## Flora and fauna



A yak at around 4,790 m (15,720 ft)

There is very little native flora or fauna on Everest. A type of [moss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moss) grows at 6,480 metres (21,260 ft) on Mount Everest and it may be the highest altitude plant species.[^cite_note-73] An alpine cushion plant called *[Arenaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arenaria_\(plant\))* is known to grow below 5,500 metres (18,000 ft) in the region.[^cite_note-autumn-74] According to the study based on satellite data from 1993 to 2018, vegetation is expanding in the Everest region. Researchers have found plants in areas that were previously deemed bare.[^cite_note-75]&#x20;

A minute species of black [jumping spider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jumping_spider), *[Euophrys omnisuperstes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euophrys_omnisuperstes)*, has been found at elevations as high as 6,700 metres (22,000 ft),[^cite_note-wanless-76] possibly making it the highest confirmed non-microscopic permanent resident on Earth. Another *Euophrys* species, *E. everestensis*, has been found at 5,030 metres (16,500 ft), and may feed on insects that have been blown there by the wind.[^cite_note-wanless-76] There is a high likelihood of microscopic life at even higher altitudes.&#x20;

The [bar-headed goose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar-headed_goose) migrates over the Himalayas and have been seen flying at the higher altitudes of the mountain.[^cite_note-his7-77] In 1953, George Lowe (part of the expedition of Tenzing and Hillary) said that he saw bar-headed geese flying over Everest's summit.[^cite_note-78] Another bird species, the [chough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chough), have been spotted as high as the [South Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Col) at 7,906 m (25,938 ft)[^cite_note-79] and [yellow-billed choughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow-billed_chough) have been seen as high as 7,900 m (25,919 ft).[^cite_note-his7-77]&#x20;

[Yaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yak) are often used to haul gear for Mount Everest climbs. They can haul around 100 kg (220 pounds), have thick fur and large lungs.[^cite_note-autumn-74] Other animals in the region include the [Himalayan tahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_tahr), which is sometimes the prey of the [snow leopard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_leopard).[^cite_note-80] The [Himalayan black bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_black_bear) can be found up to about 4,300 metres (14,000 ft) and the [red panda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_panda) is also present in the region.[^cite_note-81] One expedition found a surprising range of species in the region including a [pika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pika) and ten new species of ants.[^cite_note-82]&#x20;

### Conservation

On the Nepalese side, Everest is protected as part of [Sagarmatha National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagarmatha_National_Park),[^cite_note-83] while on the Chinese side the mountain is protected as part of [Qomolangma National Nature Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qomolangma_National_Nature_Preserve).[^cite_note-84]&#x20;

## Climate

Mount Everest has an [ice cap climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_cap_climate) (Köppen *EF*) with all months averaging well below freezing.[^cite_note-85]&#x20;

| Climate data for Mount Everest (Summit) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| Month | Jan | Feb | Mar | Apr | May | Jun | Jul | Aug | Sep | Oct | Nov | Dec | Year |
| Mean minimum °C (°F) | −36 (−33) | −35 (−31) | −32 (−26) | −31 (−24) | −25 (−13) | −20 (−4) | −18 (0) | −18 (0) | −21 (−6) | −27 (−17) | −30 (−22) | −34 (−29) | −36 (−33) |
| Source: [^cite_note-86] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

### Climate change

The icefall is moving by \~1m per day, a rate that has roughly doubled since 2009.

The base camp for Everest expeditions based out of Nepal is located by [Khumbu Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu_Glacier), which is rapidly thinning and destabilizing due to [climate change](5042951.md), making it unsafe for climbers. As recommended by the committee formed by Nepal's government to facilitate and monitor mountaineering in the Everest region, Taranath Adhikari—the director general of Nepal's tourism department—said they have plans to move the base camp to a lower altitude. This would mean a longer distance for climbers between the base camp and Camp 1. Although officials had initially stated that the move might happen by 2024,[^cite_note-87] resistance from the climbing and Sherpa communities resulted in the move being put on hold, in part because there is no viable alternative camp location that would allow climbers to pass through the icefall in the safer early morning hours.[^cite_note-88]&#x20;

### Meteorology

| [Atmospheric pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_pressure) comparison | Pressure | Reference |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [kilopascal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pascal_\(unit\)) | [psi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pounds_per_square_inch) |  |  |
| [Olympus Mons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympus_Mons) summit | 0.03 | 0.0044 | – |
| [Mars](14640471.md) average | 0.6 | 0.087 | – |
| [Hellas Planitia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellas_Planitia) bottom | 1.16 | 0.168 | – |
| [Armstrong limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armstrong_limit) | 6.25 | 0.906 | – |
| Mount Everest summit | 33.7 | 4.89 | [^cite_note-89] |
| Earth [sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level) | 101.3 | 14.69 | – |
| [Dead Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Sea) level | 106.7 | 15.48 | [^cite_note-90] |
| Surface of [Venus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus) | 9,200 | 1,330 | [^cite_note-91] |

In 2008, a new weather station at about 8,000 m (26,000 ft) elevation went online.[^cite_note-uiaa-92] The project was orchestrated by Stations at High Altitude for Research on the Environment (SHARE), which also placed the [Mount Everest webcam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Everest_webcam) in 2011.[^cite_note-uiaa-92][^cite_note-93] The solar-powered weather station is on the [South Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Col).[^cite_note-94]&#x20;

Mount Everest extends into the upper [troposphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troposphere) and penetrates the [stratosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratosphere).[^cite_note-dz-95] The air pressure at the summit is generally about one-third what it is at sea level. The altitude can expose the summit to the fast and freezing winds of the [jet stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_stream).[^cite_note-Peplow2004-96] Winds commonly attain 160 km/h (100 mph);[^cite_note-theweek-97] in February 2004, a wind speed of 280 km/h (175 mph) was recorded at the summit.&#x20;

These winds can hamper or endanger climbers, by blowing them into chasms[^cite_note-theweek-97] or (by [Bernoulli's principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernoulli%27s_principle)) by lowering the air pressure further, reducing available oxygen by up to 14 percent.[^cite_note-Peplow2004-96][^cite_note-98] To avoid the harshest winds, climbers typically aim for a 7- to 10-day window in the spring and fall when the Asian [monsoon](57630.md) season is starting up or ending.&#x20;

Mount Everest hosts several weather stations that collect important data on high-altitude weather conditions. Among them is the Balcony Station, the highest weather station on the planet, located at about 8,430 metres (27,660 ft) above sea level. Set up by climate scientists Tom Matthews and Baker Perry in 2019, this station is positioned just below the summit of Everest, which is the highest point on Earth.[^cite_note-99]&#x20;

As of January 20, 2020, the Balcony Station ceased transmitting data.[^cite_note-100]&#x20;

## Expeditions

See also: [List of people who died climbing Mount Everest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_who_died_climbing_Mount_Everest)



Climbers below the [Geneva Spur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Spur)



Reunion of the 1953 British team

Because Everest is the highest mountain in the world, it has attracted considerable attention and climbing attempts. Whether the mountain was climbed in ancient times is unknown. It may have been climbed in 1924, although this has never been confirmed, as neither man making the attempt returned. Climbing routes have been established over decades of expeditions.[^cite_note-101]&#x20;

The summit is first known to have been reached by humans in 1953.[^cite_note-la-102] Despite the effort poured into expeditions, only about 200 people had summited by 1987.[^cite_note-la-102] Everest remained a difficult climb for decades, even by professional climbers and large national expeditions, which were the norm until the commercial era began in the 1990s.[^cite_note-japtimes-103] As of December 2024, [The Himalayan Database](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Himalayan_Database) recorded just under 13,000 total summits, by around 7,200 different people.[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106]&#x20;

Although lower mountains have longer or steeper climbs, Everest is so high the [jet stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_stream) can hit it. Climbers can be faced with winds beyond 320 km/h (200 mph) when the weather shifts.[^cite_note-107] At certain times of the year the jet stream shifts north, providing periods of relative calm at the mountain.[^cite_note-wind-108] Other dangers include blizzards and avalanches.[^cite_note-wind-108]&#x20;

### Early attempts

In 1885, [Clinton Thomas Dent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Thomas_Dent), president of the [Alpine Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_Club_\(UK\)), suggested climbing Everest was possible in his book *Above the Snow Line*.[^cite_note-109] The northern approach to the mountain was discovered by [George Mallory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mallory) and [Guy Bullock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_Bullock) on the initial [1921 British Reconnaissance Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1921_British_Mount_Everest_reconnaissance_expedition). It was not equipped for a serious attempt to climb the mountain. With Mallory leading, and thus becoming the first European to set foot on Everest's flanks, they climbed the North Col to an altitude of 7,005 metres (22,982 ft). From there, Mallory espied a route to the top, but the party was unprepared to climb further and descended. The British returned for a [1922 expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1922_British_Mount_Everest_expedition). On the first summit attempt Mallory, [Col. Felix Norton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Felix_Norton), and [Howard Somervell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Somervell) without supplemental oxygen reached 8,225 m (26,985 ft), the first time a human reported to climb higher than 8,000 m (26,247 ft). [George Finch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Finch_\(chemist\)) together with [Geoffrey Bruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Bruce) climbed using oxygen for the first time. They ascended at a remarkable speed—290 metres (951 ft) per hour—and reached an altitude of 8,321 m (27,300 ft).&#x20;

The [next expedition was in 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_British_Mount_Everest_Expedition). The initial attempt by Mallory and Geoffrey Bruce was aborted when weather conditions prevented the establishment of Camp VI. The next attempt was by Norton and Somervell, who climbed without oxygen and in perfect weather, traversing the North Face into the [Great Couloir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Couloir). Norton managed to reach 8,572.8 m (28,126 ft), though he ascended only 30 m (100 ft) or so in the last hour. Mallory used oxygen equipment for a last-ditch effort. He chose young [Andrew Irvine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Irvine_\(mountaineer\)) as his partner.[^cite_note-110] On 8 June 1924, George Mallory and [Andrew Irvine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Irvine_\(mountaineer\)) made an attempt on the summit via the North Col-North Ridge-Northeast Ridge route from which they never returned. On 1 May 1999, the [Mallory and Irvine Research Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mallory_and_Irvine_Research_Expedition) found Mallory's body on the North Face in a snow basin below and to the west of the traditional site of Camp VI. Controversy has raged in the mountaineering community whether one or both reached the summit 29 years before the first confirmed ascent and safe descent in 1953. Irvine's detached foot, still in a boot and sock, was found in 2024.[^cite_note-111]&#x20;



1952 documentary

In 1933, [Lady Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucy,_Lady_Houston), a British [millionaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millionaire), funded the [Houston Everest Flight of 1933](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston%E2%80%93Mount_Everest_flight_expedition), which saw a formation of two [aeroplanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westland_Wallace) led by the [Marquess of Clydesdale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Douglas-Hamilton,_14th_Duke_of_Hamilton) fly over the Everest [summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summit_\(topography\)).[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-flymicro-113][^cite_note-114]&#x20;

Early expeditions—such as [Charles Bruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Granville_Bruce)'s in the 1920s and [Hugh Ruttledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Ruttledge)'s two unsuccessful attempts in [1933](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1933_British_Mount_Everest_expedition) and [1936](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_British_Mount_Everest_expedition)—tried to ascend the mountain from [Tibet](31516.md), via the North Face. Access was closed from the north to Western expeditions in 1950 after China took control of Tibet. In 1950, [Bill Tilman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Tilman) and a small party which included [Charles Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Snead_Houston), Oscar Houston, and Betsy Cowles undertook an [exploratory expedition to Everest through Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Everest_reconnaissance_from_Nepal#1950_Houston%E2%80%93Tilman_exploration_of_Solu_Khumbu) along the route which has become the standard approach to Everest from the south.[^cite_note-115]&#x20;

The [1952 Swiss Mount Everest expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Swiss_Mount_Everest_expedition) was granted permission to attempt a climb from Nepal. It established a route through the Khumbu icefall and ascended to the South Col at an elevation of 7,986 m (26,201 ft). [Raymond Lambert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_Lambert) and [Sherpa](224737.md) [Tenzing Norgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzing_Norgay) were able to reach an elevation of about 8,595 m (28,199 ft) on the Southeast Ridge, setting a new climbing altitude record. Tenzing's experience was useful when he was hired to be part of the British expedition in 1953.[^cite_note-116] The Swiss made another post-monsoon attempt in the autumn; they made it to the South Col but were driven back by winter winds and severe cold.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118]&#x20;

### First successful ascent by Tenzing and Hillary, 1953

Main article: [1953 British Mount Everest expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_British_Mount_Everest_expedition)



[Edmund Hillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Hillary) and [Tenzing Norgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzing_Norgay)

In 1953, a ninth British expedition, led by [John Hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hunt,_Baron_Hunt), returned to Nepal. Hunt selected two climbing pairs to attempt the summit. The first, [Tom Bourdillon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Bourdillon) and [Charles Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Evans_\(mountaineer\)), came within 100 m (330 ft) of the summit on 26 May 1953, but turned back after running into oxygen problems. As planned, their work in routefinding, breaking trail and oxygen caches were of great aid to the following pair. Two days later, the expedition made its second assault with the second pair: New Zealander [Edmund Hillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Hillary) and [Tenzing Norgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzing_Norgay), a Nepali [Sherpa](224737.md) climber. They reached the summit at 11\:30 am local time on 29 May 1953 via the South Col route. At the time, both acknowledged it as a team effort by the whole expedition, but Tenzing revealed a few years later that Hillary had put his foot on the summit first.[^cite_note-Ullman-119] They took photos and buried sweets and a small cross in the snow before descending.[^cite_note-120]&#x20;

### 1950s–60s

On 23 May 1956, [Ernst Schmied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Schmied) and [Juerg Marmet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juerg_Marmet) ascended.[^cite_note-121] [Wang Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Fuzhou), [Gonpo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gongbu_\(mountaineer\)) and Qu Yinhua of China made the first reported [ascent of the peak from the North Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_Chinese_Mount_Everest_expedition) in May 1960.[^cite_note-NorthRidge-16] The first American to climb Everest, [Jim Whittaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Whittaker), joined by [Nawang Gombu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawang_Gombu), reached the summit on 1 May 1963 on the [American Mount Everest expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_American_Mount_Everest_expedition) and on 22 May on the same expedition [Tom Hornbein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Hornbein) and [Willi Unsoeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willi_Unsoeld) were the first the traverse the mountain by climbing via the [North Face](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Face_\(Everest\)) and descending via the [South Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Col).[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123]&#x20;

### 1970s

See also: [1970 Mount Everest disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_Mount_Everest_disaster)

In 1970, Japanese mountaineers conducted a major expedition. The centrepiece was a large "siege"-style expedition, working on finding a new route up the Southwest Face.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnsworth2000594-124] Another element was an attempt to ski Everest.[^cite_note-japtimes-103] Despite a staff of over one hundred and a decade of planning, the expedition suffered eight deaths and failed to summit.[^cite_note-japtimes-103] However, Japanese expeditions enjoyed some successes. [Yuichiro Miura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuichiro_Miura) became the first man to ski down Everest from the South Col—he descended nearly 1,300 vertical metres (4,200 ft) from the South Col before falling with extreme injuries. Another success was an expedition that put four on the summit via the South Col route.[^cite_note-japtimes-103][^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Miura's exploits became the subject of film, and he went on to become the oldest person to summit Everest in 2003 aged 70 and in 2013 aged 80.[^cite_note-127]&#x20;

In 1975, [Junko Tabei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junko_Tabei) became the first woman to summit Everest.[^cite_note-japtimes-103] The [1975 British Mount Everest Southwest Face expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_British_Mount_Everest_Southwest_Face_expedition) led and organised by [Chris Bonington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bonington) made the first ascent of the Southwest Face from the [Western Cwm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Cwm). In 1978, [Reinhold Messner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinhold_Messner) and [Peter Habeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Habeler) made the first ascent of Everest without supplemental oxygen.&#x20;

### 1979/1980: Winter Himalaism



Confirmation of the summit obtained by [Nepal's Ministry of Tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Tourism_Board)

The Polish climber [Andrzej Zawada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrzej_Zawada) headed the first winter ascent of Everest, the first winter ascent of an eight-thousander. On 15 January, the team managed to set up Camp III at 7,150 metres (23,460 ft) above sea level, but further action was stopped by hurricane-force winds. The weather improved after 11 February, when [Leszek Cichy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leszek_Cichy), Walenty Fiut and [Krzysztof Wielicki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krzysztof_Wielicki) set up camp IV on [South Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Col) at 7,906 metres (25,938 ft). Cichy and Wielicki started the final ascent at 6\:50 am on 17 February. At 2\:40 pm [Andrzej Zawada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrzej_Zawada) at base camp heard the climbers' voices over the radio – "We are on the summit! The strong wind blows all the time. It is unimaginably cold."[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-theclymb.com-129][^cite_note-130] The successful winter ascent started a new decade of Winter Himalaism, which became a Polish specialisation. After 1980 Poles did ten first winter ascents on [8000 metre peaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight-thousander).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-theclymb.com-129][^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133]&#x20;

### Lho La tragedy, 1989

In 1989, Eugeniusz Chrobak led a Polish-backed international expedition to Mount Everest via the highly technical west ridge. The team included ten Polish climbers and nine international participants from countries including the United States, Mexico, Canada, and the United Kingdom. While the international members operated on separate objectives, the core Polish team focused on a difficult new variation of the route. On 24 May, Chrobak and Andrzej Marciniak, starting from camp V at 8,200 metres (26,900 ft), overcame the ridge and reached the summit. But on 27 May, during an avalanche from the side of [Khumbutse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbutse) near the [Lho La](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lho_La) pass, four climbers were killed: Mirosław Dąsal, Mirosław Gardzielewski, [Zygmunt Andrzej Heinrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zygmunt_Andrzej_Heinrich) and Wacław Otręba. The following day, due to his injuries, Chrobak died. Marciniak, who was injured, was saved by a rescue expedition in which [Artur Hajzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artur_Hajzer) and New Zealanders [Gary Ball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Ball) and [Rob Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rob_Hall) took part. The organisation of the rescue included [Reinhold Messner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinhold_Messner), [Elizabeth Hawley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Hawley), [Carlos Carsolio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Carsolio) and the US consul.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]&#x20;

### 1996 disaster

Main article: [1996 Mount Everest disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Mount_Everest_disaster)

On 10 and 11 May 1996, eight climbers died after guided expeditions were caught in a blizzard during a summit attempt on 10 May. During the 1996 season, 15 people died. These were the highest death tolls for a single weather event, and season, until the 16 deaths in the [2014 Mount Everest ice avalanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Mount_Everest_ice_avalanche). The guiding disaster gained publicity and raised questions about the commercialisation of climbing and the safety of guiding clients on Everest.&#x20;

Journalist [Jon Krakauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Krakauer), on assignment from *[Outside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outside_\(magazine\))* magazine, was in one of the affected guided parties, and published the bestseller *[Into Thin Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Into_Thin_Air)*, which related his experience. Krakauer was critical of guide [Anatoli Boukreev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatoli_Boukreev).[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-Russia_Beyond-137] A year later, Boukreev co-authored *[The Climb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Climb_\(book\))*, in part as a rebuttal of Krakauer's portrayal.[^cite_note-138] The dispute sparked debate within the climbing community. Boukreev was awarded The American Alpine Club's David Sowles Award for his rescue efforts on the expedition.[^cite_note-Russia_Beyond-137] In 2004 researchers from the [University of Toronto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Toronto) told the *[New Scientist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Scientist)* that analysis of conditions on 11 May suggested that weather caused oxygen levels to plunge about 14 per cent.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]&#x20;

One survivor was [Beck Weathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beck_Weathers), left for dead about 275 metres (900 feet) from Camp 4 at 7,950 metres (26,085 feet). After spending a night on the mountain, Weathers made it back to Camp 4 with massive frostbite and vision impaired due to snow blindness.[^cite_note-badassoftheweek.com-141] Fellow climbers considered his condition terminal and left him in a tent to die overnight.[^cite_note-DMag-142] Weathers was lowered to Camp 2 and a helicopter rescue was organised by the Nepali Army.[^cite_note-badassoftheweek.com-141][^cite_note-DMag-142] The storm's impact on climbers on the North Ridge, where several climbers also died, was detailed in a first-hand account by [Matt Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Dickinson) in his book *The Other Side of Everest*. Sixteen-year-old Mark Pfetzer was on the climb and wrote about it in, *Within Reach: My Everest Story*. The 2015 feature film *[Everest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_\(2015_film\))* is based on the events of this guiding disaster.[^cite_note-143]&#x20;

### 2006

| [Tomas Olsson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomas_Olsson) | [Sweden](5058739.md) |

| Sri Kishan[^cite_note-himalayandatabase2006-145] | [India](14533.md) |
| *\*Lhotse face fatality* |  |



Small avalanche on Everest, 2006

In 2006, 12 people died.[^cite_note-147] [David Sharp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Sharp_\(mountaineer\))'s death triggered debate and years of discussion about climbing ethics. The question was whether climbers had left a man to die and whether he could have been saved. He attempted to summit alone with two bottles of oxygen instead of the standard five used by most climbers.[^cite_note-independent_Death-144][^cite_note-top-148] He arranged his permit through [Asian Trekking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Trekking), a Kathmandu-based company that provided basic services to base camp, but not beyond that point.[^cite_note-washingtonpost-149] Sharp climbed without a radio or guide.[^cite_note-top-148] Multiple climbing parties encountered Sharp in distress.[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

Double-amputee climber [Mark Inglis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Inglis) said that on 15 May his climbing party, and many others, had passed Sharp, sheltering under a rock overhang 450 metres (1,480 ft) below the summit, without attempting a rescue.[^cite_note-151] Inglis said 40 people had passed by Sharp, but he might have been overlooked as climbers assumed Sharp was the corpse nicknamed "[Green Boots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Boots)",[^cite_note-152] but Inglis was not aware that climbers had tried to help Sharp despite being in the process of helping an injured woman, [Burçak Özoğlu Poçan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bur%C3%A7ak_%C3%96zo%C4%9Flu_Po%C3%A7an), down. There has been discussion about [Himex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himex) in the commentary on Inglis and Sharp. In regard to Inglis's initial comments, he later revised details because he had been interviewed while he was "physically and mentally exhausted, and in much pain. He had suffered frostbite – he later had five fingertips amputated."[^cite_note-thefever-153] It was estimated that Sharp summited on 14 May and began his descent, but on 15 May he was in trouble and being passed by climbers on their way up and down.[^cite_note-change-154] It is believed he was suffering from hypoxia and was about 300 m (1,000 ft) from the summit on the North Side route.[^cite_note-change-154]&#x20;

[*The Tribune*, India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tribune_\(India\)), quoted someone who described what happened to Sharp as "the most shameful act in the history of mountaineering".[^cite_note-155] Much of this controversy was captured by the *[Discovery Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_Channel)* while filming *[Everest: Beyond the Limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest:_Beyond_the_Limit)*. A crucial decision affecting Sharp is shown where a returning climber, adventurer [Maxim Chaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxim_Chaya), is descending and radios his base camp manager ([Russell Brice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Brice)) that he has found a frostbitten and unconscious climber in distress. Chaya is unable to identify Sharp, who had chosen to climb solo and did not identify himself to other climbers. The manager assumes Sharp is part of a group that has already calculated they must abandon him, and informs his lone climber there is no chance of him being able to help Sharp by himself. As Sharp's condition deteriorates and other descending climbers pass him, his opportunities for rescue diminish: his legs and feet curl from frostbite, preventing him walking; later descending climbers are lower on oxygen and lack the strength to offer aid; time runs out for Sherpas to return and rescue him. Sharp's body remained just below the summit on the Chinese side next to "Green Boots"; they shared a space in a rock cave that was an ad hoc tomb.[^cite_note-change-154] Sharp's body was removed from the cave in 2007,[^cite_note-156] and since 2014, Green Boots has been missing, presumably removed or buried.[^cite_note-Nuwer-15]&#x20;

As the Sharp debate started, on 26 May Australian climber [Lincoln Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Hall_\(climber\)) was found alive after being left.[^cite_note-\:2-157] He was found by a party of four climbers who, giving up their own attempt, stayed with Hall and descended with him and 11 Sherpas sent up to carry him down. Hall fully recovered. His team had assumed he had died from cerebral edema, and were instructed to cover him with rocks.[^cite_note-\:2-157] There were no rocks to do this and he was abandoned.[^cite_note-\:3-158] The erroneous information of his death was passed to his family.[^cite_note-\:3-158]&#x20;

### 2007

On 21 May 2007, Canadian climber [Meagan McGrath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meagan_McGrath) initiated the successful high-altitude rescue of Nepali Usha Bista. McGrath was selected as a 2011 recipient of the Sir Edmund Hillary Foundation of Canada Humanitarian Award, which recognises a Canadian who has contributed a significant service in the Himalayan Region of Nepal.[^cite_note-159]&#x20;

### Ascent statistics up to 2010 season



Ascents of Mount Everest by year through 2010



The sun rising on Everest in 2011

By the end of 2010, there had been 5,104 summits by about 3,142 individuals, with 77 per cent accomplished since 2000.[^cite_note-8000ers-160] The summit was achieved in 7 of the 22 years from 1953 to 1974 and not missed between 1975 and 2014.[^cite_note-8000ers-160] An illustration of the explosion of popularity is provided by the number of daily ascents. Analysis of the [1996 Mount Everest disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Mount_Everest_disaster) shows part of the blame was on the bottleneck caused by a large number of climbers (33 to 36) attempting to summit on the same day; this was unusually high at the time. By comparison, on 23 May 2010, the summit was reached by 169 climbers – more summits in a single day than in the 31 years from the first successful summit in 1953 to 1983.[^cite_note-8000ers-160] Nearly all attempts at the summit are done using one of two main routes. In 2005–07, more than half elected to use the more challenging, but cheaper northeast route. In 2008, the northeast route was closed by the Chinese government for the season, and the only people able to summit from the north that year were athletes [carrying the Olympic torch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Summer_Olympics_summit_of_Mt._Everest) for the [2008 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-nytimes_2008-161] The route was closed to foreigners in 2009 in the run-up to the 50th anniversary of the Dalai Lama's exile.[^cite_note-162] These closures led to declining interest in the north route, and in 2010, two-thirds summitted from the south.[^cite_note-8000ers-160]&#x20;

### 2010s

Main articles: [Mount Everest in 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Everest_in_2012) and [Mount Everest in 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Everest_in_2013)



Selfie on the summit, 2012

The 2010s were a time of new highs and lows for the mountain, with disasters in 2013 and 2014 causing record deaths. In 2015 there were no summits for the first time in decades.[^cite_note-\:0x-163] From 14–15 May 2011, Michael Horst, an American guide, summited Mount Everest and Lhotse (a "Double Crown") without descending below Camp IV (South Col), with less than 21 hours elapsing between the two summits, in the first such sub-24-hour Double Crown ascent.[^cite_note-climb_21hours-164] A record was set in 2019 with over 890 summiters.[^cite_note-time5604758-165]&#x20;

### 2014 avalanche

Main article: [2014 Mount Everest ice avalanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Mount_Everest_ice_avalanche)



The location of the fatal ice avalanche on the 2014 route, and the revised 2015 route through the Khumbu

On 18 April 2014, an avalanche hit the area just below Base Camp 2.[^cite_note-166] 16 people were killed, all Nepali guides, and nine injured.[^cite_note-167] In response, Sherpa climbing guides walked off the job and most climbing companies pulled out in respect for the Sherpa people mourning their loss.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169]&#x20;

### 2015 avalanche and earthquake

Main article: [2015 Mount Everest avalanches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Mount_Everest_avalanches)

2015 was set to be a record-breaking season of climbs, with hundreds of permits. However, on 25 April 2015, an [earthquake measuring 7.8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2015_Nepal_earthquake) [M<sub>w</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moment_magnitude_scale) triggered an avalanche that hit [Everest Base Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_Base_Camp),[^cite_note-170] shutting down the season.[^cite_note-171] 18 bodies were recovered by the Indian Army mountaineering team.[^cite_note-172] The avalanche began on [Pumori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pumori),[^cite_note-173] moved through the [Khumbu Icefall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu_Icefall) on the southwest side of Everest, and slammed into the [South Base Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_Base_Camp#South_Base_Camp_in_Nepal).[^cite_note-174] 2015 was the first time since 1974 with no spring summits, as all climbing teams pulled out.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-WillEverest-176] One reason for this was the high probability of aftershocks.[^cite_note-177] Just weeks after the first quake, the region experienced a 7.3 quake and there were [many aftershocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_aftershocks_of_April_2015_Nepal_earthquake).[^cite_note-178]&#x20;

The quakes trapped hundreds of climbers above the Khumbu icefall, and they had to be evacuated by helicopter as they ran low on supplies.[^cite_note-supplies-179] The quake shifted the route through the ice fall, making it impassable.[^cite_note-supplies-179] Bad weather made helicopter evacuation difficult.[^cite_note-supplies-179] There was a large impact overall on Nepal, with 9,000 dead.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181] In Tibet, by 28 April at least 25 had died.[^cite_note-182]&#x20;

### 2016 and 2017

Main articles: [Mount Everest in 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Everest_in_2016) and [Mount Everest in 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Everest_in_2017)

Hawley's database records 641 made it to the summit in early 2016.[^cite_note-191] 2017 was the biggest season yet, yielding hundreds of summiters and a handful of deaths.[^cite_note-192] On 27 May, [Kami Rita](54159070.md) made his 21st summit with the Alpine Ascents Everest Expedition, one of three people, along with [Apa Sherpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apa_Sherpa) and [Phurba Tashi Sherpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phurba_Tashi) to make it to the summit 21 times.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] The season had a tragic start with the death of [Ueli Steck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ueli_Steck), who died from a fall during a warm-up climb.[^cite_note-195] There was continued discussion about possible changes to the [Hillary Step](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Step).[^cite_note-196] Summiters for 2017 was tallied up to be 648.[^cite_note-telegraph.co.uk1-188]&#x20;

### 2018

Main article: [Mount Everest in 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Everest_in_2018)

A record 807 summited in 2018.[^cite_note-\:4-197] One factor that aided this was an especially long and clear weather window of 11 days during the critical spring climbing season.[^cite_note-\:0x-163][^cite_note-auto5-198][^cite_note-\:0-189] Various records were broken, including a summit by double-amputee [Xia Boyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xia_Boyu), after winning a case in the Nepali Supreme Court. Seven climbers died.[^cite_note-\:0x-163] Although record numbers summitted, old-time summiteers that made expeditions in the 1980s lamented the crowding, feces, and cost.[^cite_note-auto5-198]&#x20;

### 2019

| 2019 fatalities[^cite_note-upi.com-199] |  |
| --- | --- |

| Anjali S. Kulkarni | [India](14533.md) |
| Ernst Landgraf | [Austria](26964606.md) |

| Ravi Thakar | [India](14533.md) |
