| *\*Declared dead after missing* |  |

The spring or pre-monsoon window for 2019 witnessed the deaths of climbers. Images of hundreds queuing to reach the summit and reports of climbers stepping over dead bodies dismayed people around the world.[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203]&#x20;

There was an announcement of an expedition to re-measure the height of Everest, particularly in light of the 2015 earthquakes.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205][^cite_note-bbc_2019-206] Among the climbing teams was a scientific expedition with a planned study of pollution, and how things like snow and vegetation influence the availability of food and water in the region.[^cite_note-207] In the 2019 spring season, there were roughly 40 teams with almost 400 climbers and several hundred guides attempting to summit on the Nepali side.[^cite_note-nbcrita-208][^cite_note-cnnrita-209][^cite_note-210] Nepal issued 381 climbing permits for 2019.[^cite_note-\:4-197] For the northern routes in Chinese Tibet, several hundred more permits were issued.[^cite_note-211] In May, Nepali mountaineering guide Kami Rita summited twice within a week, his 23rd and 24th ascents.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-nbcrita-208][^cite_note-cnnrita-209]&#x20;

By 28 May, the death toll reached 11, when a climber died at about 7,900 m (26,000 ft) during the descent,[^cite_note-upi.com-199] and a 12th climber was missing and later declared dead.[^cite_note-Slater-200] The deaths were possibly due to crowding leading to delays high on the mountain, and shorter weather windows.[^cite_note-\:4-197] Someone who had summited previously noted that when the weather window opens, long lines form as everyone rushes to summit and get back down.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] Despite the number of deaths, reports indicated that a record 891 climbers summited in the spring 2019 climbing season.[^cite_note-\:6-215][^cite_note-time5604758-165] Although China has had permit restrictions, and Nepal requires a doctor to sign off on climbing permits,[^cite_note-\:6-215] the natural dangers of climbing such as falls and avalanches combined with medical issues aggravated by Everest's extreme altitude led to 2019 being a year with a comparatively high death toll.[^cite_note-\:6-215]&#x20;

On 23 May, [Juan Pablo Mohr Prieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Pablo_Mohr_Prieto) became the first Chilean to ascend both Lhotse and Everest without supplemental oxygen, taking 164 hours and inventing a new Guinness World Record.[^cite_note-Stephenson_2021-216] On 27 May, [Mingma Dorchi Sherpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mingma_Dorchi_Sherpa) set the world record for fastest ever Double Crown ascent of Lhotse and Everest, with a traverse time between the summits of 6 hours and 1 minute, using supplemental oxygen.[^cite_note-guinness_world_records_2020-07-08-217]&#x20;

### 2020s

Both Nepal and China prohibited foreign climbing groups during the 2020 season, due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md). 2020 saw no summits from the Nepal (South) Side.[^cite_note-nationalgeographic_covid-190]&#x20;

In October 2025, blizzard-like conditions prompted Chinese authorities to evacuate nearly 350 people by October 6, with about 200 more stranded on the Everest Scenic Area.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219]&#x20;

In May 2026 Nepali climber [Kami Rita](54159070.md), age 56, broke his personal record ascending the mountain for the 32nd time, when at the same time [Lhakpa Sherpa](50511396.md) made her record 11th time to the summit.[^cite_note-dw-220] That same month, [Nirmal Purja](60887613.md) set the record for fastest Double Crown traverse, summitting both Everest and Lhotse without supplemental oxygen, at 13 hours and 42 minutes.[^cite_note-Chronicle_editors_2026-06-02-221] Chinese climber Gexi Luori made the first ever quadruple ascent of Everest and Lhotse, summitting the peaks twice each, in the same season.[^cite_note-Luori_quadruple_ascent-222]&#x20;

## Climbing

### Permits

| Location | Altitude (km) |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Summit 8848 m / 29035 ft | 8.8 |  |
| Camp 4 8000 m / 26000 ft | 8.0 |  |
| Camp 3 6800 m / 22300 ft | 6.8 |  |
| Camp 2 6400 m / 21000 ft | 6.4 |  |
| Camp 1 6100 m / 20000 ft | 6.1 |  |
| Base camp 5400 m / 17700 ft | 5.4 |  |



Looking up along the southern ridgeline in 2010, the face of the [Hillary Step](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Step) is visible. The top of the Southwest Face is on the left in shadow, and to the right is the top of the East/Kangshung Face. In 2016 and 2017 there were reports the Hillary Step was changed, which triggered discussion in the climbing community.

In 2014, Nepal issued 334 climbing permits, which were extended until 2019 due to the closure.[^cite_note-ekantipur1-224] In 2015, Nepal issued 357 permits, but the mountain was closed again because of the avalanche and earthquake, and these permits were given a two-year extension to 2017.[^cite_note-kathmandupost.ekantipur.com-225][^cite_note-ekantipur1-224]&#x20;

In 2017, a person who tried to climb Everest without the \$11,000 permit was caught after he made it past the Khumbu icefall. He faced, among other penalties, a \$22,000 fine and a possible four years in jail. In the end, he was allowed to return home but banned from mountaineering in Nepal for 10 years.[^cite_note-226]&#x20;

The number of permits issued each year by Nepal is:[^cite_note-kathmandupost.ekantipur.com-225][^cite_note-227]&#x20;

- 2008: 160

- 2009: 220

- 2010: 209

- 2011: 225

- 2012: 208

- 2013: 316

- 2014: 326 (extended for use through 2019)

- 2015: 356 (extended for use through 2017)

- 2016: 289

- 2017: 366 to 373

- 2018: 346

- 2019: 381

- 2020: 0 (no permits issued during the pandemic)

- 2021: 408 (current record)

  [^cite_note-228]

  [^cite_note-229]

- 2026: 492 (new record)

  [^cite_note-dw-220]

The Chinese side in Tibet is also managed with permits for summiting Everest.[^cite_note-230] They did not issue permits in 2008, due to the [Olympic torch relay being taken to the summit of Mount Everest.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Summer_Olympics_summit_of_Mt._Everest)[^cite_note-nytimes_2008-161]&#x20;

In March 2020, the governments of China and Nepal cancelled all climbing permits for Mount Everest due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md).[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] In April 2020, a group of Chinese mountaineers began an expedition from the Chinese side. The mountain remained closed on the Chinese side to all foreign climbers.[^cite_note-233] On 10 May 2021, a separation line was announced by Chinese authorities to prevent the spread of coronavirus from climbers ascending Nepal's side.[^cite_note-234]&#x20;

In April 2025, the Government of Nepal introduced a new regulation requiring climbers to have previously summited at least one 7,000-metre peak within Nepal before being eligible to obtain a permit for an expedition to Mount Everest. The rule is scheduled to come into effect from the Spring 2026 climbing season.[^cite_note-235]&#x20;

### Commercial climbing



Top down view showing the location of the summit, and its three main faces/sides



[Everest Base Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_base_camps)



[Gorak Shep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorakshep) is about a three-hour walk to South EBC (Everest Base Camp).

[^cite_note-236]

According to [Jon Krakauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Krakauer), the era of [commercialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercialization) of Everest started in 1985, when the summit was reached by a guided expedition led by [David Breashears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Breashears) that included [Richard Bass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Bass), a wealthy 55-year-old businessman and an amateur mountain climber with four years of climbing experience.[^cite_note-237] By the early-1990s, several companies were offering guided tours to the mountain. [Rob Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rob_Hall), one of the mountaineers who died in the [1996 disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Mount_Everest_disaster), had successfully guided 39 clients to the summit before that incident.[^cite_note-Krakauer-1997-238]<sup>: 24, 42 </sup>&#x20;

By 2016, most guiding services cost between US\$35,000 and US\$200,000.[^cite_note-heraldsun.com.au-239] Going with a "celebrity guide", usually a well-known mountaineer typically with decades of climbing experience and perhaps several Everest summits, can cost over £100,000 as of 2015.[^cite_note-240] However, the services offered vary widely and it is "buyer beware" when doing deals in Nepal, one of the poorest and least developed countries in the world.[^cite_note-heraldsun.com.au-239][^cite_note-241] Tourism contributed 7.9 per cent of the [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP) in 2019[^cite_note-242] in a country with high unemployment,[^cite_note-243] but an Everest porter can make nearly double the nation's average wage in a region in which other sources of income are lacking.[^cite_note-244]&#x20;

Costs beyond the guiding service can vary widely. It is technically possible to reach the summit with minimal additional expenses, and there are "budget" travel agencies that offer logistical support for such trips. A limited support service, offering only some meals at base camp and bureaucratic overhead like a permit, can cost as little as US\$7,000 as of 2007.[^cite_note-thefever-153] However, this is considered difficult and dangerous (as illustrated by the case of [David Sharp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Sharp_\(mountaineer\))).[^cite_note-washingtonpost-149]&#x20;

Climbing gear required to reach the summit may cost in excess of US\$8,000, and most climbers also use [bottled oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bottled_oxygen_\(climbing\)), which adds around US\$3,000. The permit to enter the Everest area from the south via Nepal costs US\$10,000 to US\$30,000 per person, depending on the size of the team. The ascent typically starts at one of the [two base camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_base_camps) near the mountain, both of which are approximately 100 kilometres (60 mi) from [Kathmandu](17168.md) and 300 kilometres (190 mi) from [Lhasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhasa) (the two nearest cities with major airports). Transferring one's equipment from the airport to the base camp may add as much as US\$2,000.[^cite_note-ClimbingMagazineCost-245]&#x20;

Many climbers hire "full service" guide companies, which provide a wide spectrum of services, including the acquisition of permits, transportation to/from base camp, food, tents, [fixed ropes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed_ropes),[^cite_note-246] medical assistance while on the mountain, an experienced mountaineer guide, and even personal porters to carry one's backpack and cook one's meals. The cost of such a guide service may range from US\$40,000 to \$80,000 per person.[^cite_note-247] Since most equipment is moved by [Sherpas](224737.md), clients of full-service guide companies can often keep their backpack weights under 10 kilograms (22 lb), or hire a Sherpa to carry their backpack for them. By contrast, climbers attempting less commercialised peaks, like [Denali](207247.md), are often expected to carry backpacks over 30 kilograms (66 lb) and, occasionally, to tow a [sled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sled) with 35 kilograms (77 lb) of gear and food.[^cite_note-248]&#x20;

The degree of commercialisation of Mount Everest is a frequent subject of criticism.[^cite_note-NG-20130600-183] [Jamling Tenzing Norgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamling_Tenzing_Norgay), the son of [Tenzing Norgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzing_Norgay), said in a 2003 interview that his late father would have been shocked to discover that rich thrill-seekers with no climbing experience were now routinely reaching the summit, "You still have to climb this mountain yourself with your feet. But the spirit of adventure is not there any more. It is lost. There are people going up there who have no idea how to put on [crampons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crampons). They are climbing because they have paid someone \$65,000. It is very selfish. It endangers the lives of others."[^cite_note-249]&#x20;

One example of this is [Shriya Shah-Klorfine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shriya_Shah-Klorfine), who had to be taught how to put on crampons during her summit attempt in 2012.[^cite_note-250] She paid at least US\$40,000 to a new guiding company for the trip, and died when she ran out of oxygen during the descent after climbing for 27 hours straight.[^cite_note-251]&#x20;

[Reinhold Messner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinhold_Messner) concurred in 2004:&#x20;

> You could die in each climb and that meant you were responsible for yourself. We were real mountaineers: careful, aware and even afraid. By climbing mountains we were not learning how big we were. We were finding out how breakable, how weak and how full of fear we are. You can only get this if you expose yourself to high danger. I have always said that a mountain without danger is not a mountain....High altitude alpinism has become tourism and show. These commercial trips to Everest, they are still dangerous. But the guides and organisers tell clients, 'Don't worry, it's all organised.' The route is prepared by hundreds of Sherpas. Extra oxygen is available in all camps, right up to the summit. People will cook for you and lay out your beds. Clients feel safe and don't care about the risks.[^cite_note-252]

By 2015, Nepal was considering requiring that climbers have some experience, hoping this would both make the mountain safer and increase revenue.[^cite_note-253] One barrier to this is that low-budget firms make money *not* taking inexperienced climbers to the summit.[^cite_note-heraldsun.com.au-239] Those turned away by Western firms can often find another firm willing to take them for a price—that they return home soon after arriving after base camp, or part way up the mountain.[^cite_note-heraldsun.com.au-239]&#x20;

&#x20;However, not all opinions on the subject among prominent mountaineers have been strictly negative. For example, [Edmund Hillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Hillary) stated in 2003 that while "Having people pay \$65,000 and then be led up the mountain by a couple of experienced guides...isn't really mountaineering at all",[^cite_note-254] he was pleased by the changes brought to Everest area by Westerners:&#x20;

> I don't have any regrets because I worked very hard indeed to improve the condition for the local people. When we first went in there they didn't have any schools, they didn't have any medical facilities, all over the years we have established 27 schools, we have two hospitals and a dozen medical clinics and then we've built bridges over wild mountain rivers and put in fresh water pipelines so in cooperation with the Sherpas we've done a lot to benefit them.[^cite_note-255]

One of the early guided summiters, Richard Bass (of [Seven Summits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Summits) fame) stated in 2003 that "Climbers should have high altitude experience before they attempt the really big mountains. People don't realise the difference between a 20,000-foot \[6,100 m] mountain and 29,000-foot \[8,800 m]. It's not just arithmetic. The reduction of oxygen in the air is proportionate to the altitude alright, but the effect on the human body is disproportionate—an exponential curve. People climb Denali \[6,190 m or 20,320 ft] or Aconcagua \[6,960 m or 22,834 ft] and think, 'Heck, I feel great up here, I'm going to try Everest.' But it's not like that."[^cite_note-256]&#x20;

#### Speed climbing

##### Altitude tents

Main article: [Altitude tent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_tent)

Some expedition teams have clients use altitude tents to pre-acclimatise prior to leaving for the mountain. Compared to traditional Everest expeditions that last 50 to 60 days, altitude tents can reduce the expedition time frame to 30 to 35 days.[^cite_note-257]&#x20;

##### Xenon gas

Further information: [Xenon § Sports doping and mountaineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenon#Sports_doping_and_mountaineering)

In 2025, four men climbed the mountain in one week. They claimed their inhalation of [xenon gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenon_gas) 10 days prior to stimulate [erythropoietin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erythropoietin) production had eliminated the need for [altitude acclimatisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_acclimatisation) over several weeks. The [International Climbing and Mountaineering Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Climbing_and_Mountaineering_Federation) (UIAA) criticised the decision, citing that there is no evidence that the inhalation of xenon improves performance in high elevation environments. Furthermore, the UIAA warned that as an anesthetic, xenon gas could result in impaired brain function, respiratory compromise, and death if used in an unmonitored setting.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260]&#x20;

#### Summiting with disabilities

Summiting Everest with disabilities has become popular in the 21st century. Sudarshan Gautam, a Canadian double-amputee, summited the mountain in 2013.[^cite_note-261] In the same year, Eli Reimer, a teenager with [Down syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Down_syndrome), hiked to the South Base Camp as part of a fundraising effort for his father's foundation.[^cite_note-262]&#x20;

### Routes



Overview South Col route and North Col/Ridge route

Mount Everest has two main climbing routes, the Southeast Ridge from [Nepal](171166.md) and the North Ridge from [Tibet](31516.md), as well as many other less frequently climbed routes.[^cite_note-evhist-263] Of the two main routes, the Southeast Ridge is technically easier and more frequently used. It was the route used by [Edmund Hillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Hillary) and [Tenzing Norgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzing_Norgay) in 1953 and the first recognised of 15 routes to the top by 1996.[^cite_note-evhist-263] This was, however, a route decision dictated more by politics than by design, as the Chinese border was closed to the western world in the 1950s, after [Annexation of Tibet by the People's Republic of China](14615536.md).[^cite_note-264]&#x20;

Most attempts are made during May, before the summer [monsoon](57630.md) season. As the monsoon season approaches, the [jet stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_stream) shifts northward, thereby reducing the average wind speeds high on the mountain.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] While attempts are sometimes made in September and October, after the monsoons, when the jet stream is again temporarily pushed northward, the additional snow deposited by the monsoons and the less stable weather patterns at the monsoons' tail end makes climbing extremely difficult.&#x20;

#### Southeast Ridge

The ascent via the Southeast Ridge begins with a trek to [Base Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everest_Base_Camp) at 5,380 m (17,700 ft) on the south side of Everest, in Nepal. Expeditions usually fly into [Lukla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lukla) (2,860 m) from [Kathmandu](17168.md) and pass through [Namche Bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namche_Bazaar). Climbers then hike to Base Camp, which usually takes six to eight days, allowing for proper altitude acclimatisation in order to prevent [altitude sickness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_sickness).[^cite_note-267] Climbing equipment and supplies are carried by [yaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yak), [dzopkyos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzo) (yak-cow hybrids), and human [porters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porter_\(carrier\)) to Base Camp on the [Khumbu Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu_Glacier). When Hillary and Tenzing climbed Everest in 1953, the British expedition they were part of (comprising over 400 climbers, porters, and Sherpas at that point) started from the [Kathmandu Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathmandu_Valley), as there were no roads further east at that time.&#x20;

Climbers spend a couple of weeks in Base Camp, acclimatising to the altitude. During that time, [Sherpas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherpa_\(people\)) and some expedition climbers set up ropes and ladders in the treacherous [Khumbu Icefall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu_Icefall).&#x20;

[Seracs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serac), [crevasses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crevasse), and shifting blocks of ice make the icefall one of the most dangerous sections of the route. Many climbers and Sherpas have been killed in this section. To reduce the hazard, climbers usually begin their ascent well before dawn, when the freezing temperatures glue ice blocks in place.&#x20;

Above the icefall is Camp I at 6,065 metres (19,900 ft).&#x20;



Climber traversing Khumbu Icefall

From Camp I, climbers make their way up the [Western Cwm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Cwm) to the base of the [Lhotse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhotse) Face, where Camp II or Advanced Base Camp (ABC) is established at 6,500 m (21,300 ft). The Western Cwm is a flat, gently rising glacial valley, marked by huge lateral crevasses in the centre, which prevent direct access to the upper reaches of the Cwm. Climbers are forced to cross on the far right, near the base of [Nuptse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuptse), to a small passageway known as the "Nuptse corner". The Western Cwm is also called the "Valley of Silence" as the topography of the area generally cuts off wind from the climbing route. The [high altitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_altitude) and a clear, windless day can make the Western Cwm unbearably hot for climbers.[^cite_note-nova-climb-268]&#x20;

From Camp II, climbers ascend the Lhotse Face on [fixed ropes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed_rope), up to Camp III, located on a small ledge at 7,470 m (24,500 ft). From there, it is another 500 metres to Camp IV on the [South Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Col) at 7,920 m (26,000 ft).&#x20;

From Camp III to Camp IV, climbers are faced with two additional challenges: the [Geneva Spur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Spur) and the [Yellow Band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_Band). The Geneva Spur is an anvil-shaped rib of black rock named by the [1952 Swiss expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Swiss_Mount_Everest_Expedition). Fixed ropes assist climbers in [scrambling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scrambling) over this snow-covered rock band. The Yellow Band is a section of interlayered [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble), [phyllite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phyllite), and [semischist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schist), which also requires about 100 metres of rope for traversing it.[^cite_note-nova-climb-268]&#x20;

On the [South Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Col), climbers enter the [death zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_zone). Climbers making summit bids typically can endure no more than two or three days at this altitude. If the weather is not clear with low winds during these short few days, climbers are forced to descend, many all the way back down to Base Camp.&#x20;

From Camp IV, climbers begin their summit push around midnight, with hopes of reaching the summit (still another 1,000 metres above) within 10 to 12 hours. Climbers first reach "The Balcony" at 8,400 m (27,600 ft), a small platform where they can rest and gaze at peaks to the south and east in the early light of dawn. Continuing up the ridge, climbers are then faced with a series of imposing rock steps which usually forces them to the east into the waist-deep snow, a serious [avalanche](73321.md) hazard. At 8,750 m (28,700 ft), a small table-sized dome of ice and snow marks the [South Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Summit_\(Everest\)).[^cite_note-nova-climb-268]&#x20;

From the South Summit, climbers follow the knife-edge Southeast Ridge along what is known as the "Cornice traverse", where snow clings to intermittent rock. This is the most exposed section of the climb, and a misstep to the left would send one 2,400 m (7,900 ft) down the Southwest Face, while to the immediate right is the 3,050 m (10,010 ft) [Kangshung Face](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangshung_Face). At the end of this traverse is an imposing 12 m (39 ft) rock wall, the [Hillary Step](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Step), at 8,790 m (28,840 ft).[^cite_note-269]&#x20;

Hillary and Tenzing were the first climbers to ascend this step, and did so using primitive ice climbing equipment and ropes. Nowadays, climbers ascend using fixed ropes previously set up by Sherpas. Once above the step, it is a comparatively easy climb to the top on moderately angled snow slopes—though the [exposure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exposure_\(heights\)) on the ridge is extreme, especially while traversing large cornices of snow. With increasing numbers climbing the mountain, the Step has frequently become a bottleneck, with climbers forced to wait significant amounts of time for their turn on the ropes, leading to problems in getting climbers efficiently up and down the mountain.&#x20;

After the Hillary Step, climbers must traverse a loose and rocky section that has a large entanglement of fixed ropes that can be troublesome in bad weather. Climbers typically spend less than half an hour at the summit to allow time to descend to Camp IV before darkness sets in, to avoid serious problems with afternoon weather, or because supplemental oxygen tanks run out.&#x20;

#### North Ridge route

See also: [Three Steps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Steps)



Mount Everest North Face from [Rongbuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rongbuk_Monastery) in Tibet

The North Ridge route begins from the north side of Everest, in [Tibet](31516.md). Expeditions trek to the [Rongbuk Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rongbuk_Glacier), setting up base camp at 5,180 m (16,990 ft) on a gravel plain just below the glacier. To reach Camp II, climbers ascend the medial moraine of the east Rongbuk Glacier up to the base of [Changtse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changtse), at around 6,100 m (20,000 ft). Camp III (ABC – Advanced Base Camp) is situated below the [North Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Col) at 6,500 m (21,300 ft). To reach Camp IV on the North Col, climbers ascend the glacier to the foot of the col where fixed ropes are used to reach the North Col at 7,010 m (23,000 ft). From the North Col, climbers ascend the rocky North Ridge to set up Camp V at around 7,775 m (25,500 ft). The route crosses the North Face in a diagonal climb to the base of the Yellow Band, reaching the site of Camp VI at 8,230 m (27,000 ft). From Camp VI, climbers make their final summit push.&#x20;

Climbers face a treacherous traverse from the base of the First Step: ascending from 8,501 to 8,534 m (27,890 to 28,000 ft), to the crux of the climb, the Second Step, ascending from 8,577 to 8,626 m (28,140 to 28,300 ft). (The Second Step includes a climbing aid called the "Chinese ladder", a metal ladder placed semi-permanently in 1975 by a party of Chinese climbers.[^cite_note-270] It has been almost continuously in place since, and ladders have been used by virtually all climbers on the route.) Once above the Second Step the inconsequential Third Step is clambered over, ascending from 8,690 to 8,800 m (28,510 to 28,870 ft). Once above these steps, the summit pyramid is climbed by a snow slope of 50 degrees, to the final summit ridge along which the top is reached.[^cite_note-271]&#x20;

### Summit



A view from the summit of Mount Everest in May 2013

The summit of Everest has been described as "the size of a dining room table".[^cite_note-272] The summit is capped with snow over ice over rock, and the layer of snow varies from year to year.[^cite_note-273] The rock summit is made of Ordovician [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone) and is a low-grade metamorphic rock.[^cite_note-274] (See the [Surveys](#Surveys) section for more on its height and about the Everest rock summit.)&#x20;

Below the summit, there is an area known as "rainbow valley", filled with dead bodies still wearing brightly coloured winter gear. Down to about 8,000 m (26,000 ft) is an area commonly called the "death zone", due to the high danger and low oxygen because of the low pressure.[^cite_note-dz-95]&#x20;

### Death zone

See also: [Effects of high altitude on humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_high_altitude_on_humans)



The summit of Mount Everest from the North side



From [Kala Patthar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kala_Patthar), Nepal

At the higher regions of Mount Everest, climbers seeking the summit typically spend substantial time within the [death zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_zone) (altitudes higher than 8,000 m or 26,000 ft), and face significant challenges to survival. Temperatures can dip to very low levels, resulting in [frostbite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frostbite) of any body part exposed to the air. Since temperatures are so low, snow is well-frozen in certain areas and death or injury by slipping and falling can occur. High winds at these altitudes on Everest are also a potential threat to climbers.&#x20;

Another significant [threat to climbers is low atmospheric pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breathing#Effects_of_ambient_air_pressure). The atmospheric pressure at the top of Everest is about a third of sea level pressure or 0.333 standard atmospheres (337 mbar), resulting in the availability of only about a third as much oxygen to breathe.[^cite_note-275]&#x20;

A sea-level dweller exposed to the atmospheric conditions at the altitude above 8,500 m (27,900 ft) without acclimatisation would likely lose consciousness within two to three minutes.[^cite_note-276] At sea level, [blood oxygen saturation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen_saturation_\(medicine\)#Definition) is generally 98 to 99 per cent. At base camp, blood saturation fell to between 85 and 87 per cent. Blood samples taken at the summit indicated very low oxygen levels in the blood. A side effect of low blood oxygen is a greatly increased breathing rate, often 80–90 breaths per minute as opposed to a more typical 20–30. Exhaustion can occur merely by attempting to breathe.[^cite_note-277]&#x20;

Lack of oxygen, exhaustion, extreme cold, and climbing hazards all contribute to the death toll. An injured person who cannot walk is in serious trouble, since rescue by helicopter is generally impractical and carrying the person off the mountain is very risky. People who die during the climb are typically left behind. As of 2015, over 200 bodies remain on the mountain.[^cite_note-Nuwer-15]&#x20;

> Debilitating symptoms consistent with high altitude cerebral oedema commonly present during descent from the summit of Mount Everest. Profound fatigue and late times in reaching the summit are early features associated with subsequent death.

— Mortality on Mount Everest, 1921–2006: descriptive study[^cite_note-278]

A 2008 study noted that the "death zone" is indeed where most Everest deaths occur, but also noted that most deaths occur during descent from the summit.[^cite_note-279] A 2014 article in *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic)* about deaths on Everest noted that while falling is one of the greatest dangers the death zone presents for all 8000ers, avalanches are a more common cause of death at lower altitudes.[^cite_note-280]&#x20;

Despite this, Everest is safer for climbers than a number of peaks by some measurements, but it depends on the period.[^cite_note-safer-281] Some examples are [Kangchenjunga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangchenjunga), [K2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K2), [Annapurna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annapurna_Massif), [Nanga Parbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanga_Parbat), and the [Eiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eiger) (especially the *nordwand*).[^cite_note-safer-281] Some factors that affect total mountain lethality include the level of popularity of the mountain, the skill of those climbing, and the difficulty of the climb.[^cite_note-282]&#x20;

Another health hazard is [retinal haemorrhages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retinal_haemorrhage), which can damage eyesight and cause blindness.[^cite_note-eyes-283] Up to a quarter of Everest climbers can experience retinal haemorrhages, and although they usually heal within weeks of returning to lower altitudes, in 2010 a climber went blind and died in the death zone.[^cite_note-eyes-283]&#x20;

The team made a huge effort for the next 12 hours to try to get him down the mountain, but to no avail, as they were unsuccessful in getting him through the difficult sections.[^cite_note-theweek1-284] Even for the able, Everest's Northeast Ridge is recognised as a challenge. It is hard to rescue someone who has become incapacitated and it can be beyond the ability of rescuers to save *anyone* in such a difficult spot.[^cite_note-theweek1-284] One way around this situation was pioneered by two Nepali men in 2011, who had intended to paraglide off the summit. They had no choice and were forced to go through with their plan anyway, because they had run out of bottled oxygen and supplies.[^cite_note-abcnews1-285] They successfully launched off the summit and para-glided down to [Namche Bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namche_Bazaar) in just 42 minutes, without having to climb down the mountain.[^cite_note-abcnews1-285]&#x20;

### Supplemental oxygen



Climber at the summit wearing an oxygen mask



Available oxygen at Everest

Most expeditions use [oxygen masks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen_mask) and [tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bottled_oxygen) above 8,000 m (26,247 ft).[^cite_note-286] Everest can be climbed without supplementary oxygen, but only by the most accomplished mountaineers and at increased risk. Low oxygen impairs cognition, and the combination of extreme weather, low temperatures, and steep slopes often requires quick, accurate decisions. While about 95 per cent of climbers who reach the summit use [bottled oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bottled_oxygen) in order to reach the top, about five per cent of climbers have summited Everest without supplemental oxygen. The death rate is double for those who attempt to reach the summit without supplemental oxygen.[^cite_note-287] Travelling above 8,000 m (26,000 ft) altitude is a factor in [cerebral hypoxia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerebral_hypoxia).[^cite_note-thom-288] One study found that Mount Everest may be the highest an acclimatised human could go, but also found that climbers may suffer permanent neurological damage despite returning to lower altitudes.[^cite_note-thom-288] The use of bottled oxygen to ascend Mount Everest has been controversial.[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291]&#x20;

[George Mallory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mallory) described the use of such oxygen as unsportsmanlike, but he later concluded that it would be impossible for him to summit without it and consequently used it on his final attempt in 1924.[^cite_note-292] When [Tenzing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzing_Norgay) and Hillary made the first successful summit in 1953, they also used [open-circuit bottled oxygen sets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bottled_oxygen_\(climbing\)), with the expedition's physiologist [Griffith Pugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griffith_Pugh) referring to the oxygen debate as a "futile controversy", noting that oxygen "greatly increases subjective appreciation of the surroundings, which after all is one of the chief reasons for climbing."[^cite_note-293] For the next twenty-five years, bottled oxygen was considered standard for any successful summit.&#x20;

> ...although an acclimatised lowlander can survive for a time on the summit of Everest without supplemental oxygen, one is so close to the limit that even a modicum of excess exertion may impair brain function.

— Thomas F. Hornbein in *The high-altitude brain*[^cite_note-thom-288]

[Reinhold Messner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinhold_Messner) was the first climber to break the bottled oxygen tradition and in 1978, with [Peter Habeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Habeler), made the first successful climb without it. In 1980, Messner summited the mountain solo, without supplemental oxygen or any porters or climbing partners, on the more difficult northwest route. Once the climbing community was satisfied that the mountain could be climbed without supplemental oxygen, many purists then took the next logical step of insisting that is how it *should* be climbed.[^cite_note-Krakauer-1997-238]<sup>: 154 </sup>&#x20;

The aftermath of the [1996 disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Mount_Everest_disaster) further intensified the debate. [Jon Krakauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Krakauer)'s *[Into Thin Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Into_Thin_Air)* (1997) expressed the author's personal criticisms of the use of bottled oxygen. Krakauer wrote that the use of bottled oxygen allowed otherwise unqualified climbers to attempt to summit, leading to dangerous situations and more deaths. The disaster was partially caused by the sheer number of climbers (34 on that day) attempting to ascend, causing bottlenecks at the Hillary Step and delaying many climbers, most of whom summited after the usual 14\:00 turnaround time. He proposed banning bottled oxygen except for emergency cases, arguing that this would both decrease the growing pollution on Everest—many bottles have accumulated on its slopes—and keep marginally qualified climbers off the mountain.&#x20;

The 1996 disaster also introduced the issue of the guide's role in using bottled oxygen.[^cite_note-294]&#x20;

Guide [Anatoli Boukreev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatoli_Boukreev)'s decision not to use bottled oxygen was sharply criticised by Jon Krakauer. Boukreev's supporters (who include G. Weston DeWalt, who co-wrote *[The Climb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Climb_\(book\))*) state that using bottled oxygen gives a false sense of security.[^cite_note-295] Krakauer and his supporters point out that, without bottled oxygen, Boukreev could not directly help his clients descend.[^cite_note-296]&#x20;

The low oxygen can cause a mental fog-like impairment of cognitive abilities described as "delayed and lethargic thought process, clinically defined as bradypsychia" even after returning to lower altitudes.[^cite_note-altitude-297] In severe cases, climbers can experience hallucinations. Some studies have found that high-altitude climbers, including Everest climbers, experience altered brain structure.[^cite_note-altitude-297]&#x20;

### Autumn climbing



Everest in September 2006

Although generally less popular than spring, Mount Everest has also been climbed in the autumn (also called the "post-monsoon season").[^cite_note-autumn-74][^cite_note-fall-298] For example, in 2010 Eric Larsen and five Nepali guides summited Everest in the autumn for the first time in ten years.[^cite_note-fall-298] The autumn season, when the monsoon ends, is regarded as more dangerous because there is typically a lot of new snow which can be unstable.[^cite_note-wind-108] However, this increased snow can make it more popular with certain winter sports like skiing and snowboarding.[^cite_note-autumn-74] Two Japanese climbers also summited in October 1973.[^cite_note-299]&#x20;

Chris Chandler and Bob Cormack summited Everest in October 1976 as part of the American Bicentennial Everest Expedition that year, the first Americans to make an autumn ascent of Mount Everest according to the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)*.[^cite_note-300] By the 21st century, summer and autumn can be more popular with skiing and snowboard attempts on Mount Everest.[^cite_note-301] During the 1980s, climbing in autumn was actually more popular than in spring.[^cite_note-302] U.S. astronaut [Karl Gordon Henize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Gordon_Henize) died in October 1993 on an autumn expedition, conducting an experiment on radiation. The amount of [background radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Background_radiation) increases with higher altitudes.[^cite_note-303]&#x20;

The mountain has also been climbed in the winter, but that is not popular because of the combination of cold high winds and shorter days.[^cite_note-304] By January the peak is typically battered by 270 km/h (170 mph) winds and the average temperature of the summit is around −33 °F (−36 °C).[^cite_note-autumn-74]&#x20;

### Thefts and crime

Some climbers have reported life-threatening thefts from supply caches. In May 2006, [Vitor Negrete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitor_Negrete), the first Brazilian to climb Everest without oxygen and part of David Sharp's party, died during his descent, and theft of gear and food from his high-altitude camp may have contributed.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306] In addition to theft, Michael Kodas describes in his book, *High Crimes: The Fate of Everest in an Age of Greed* (2008):[^cite_note-307] unethical guides and Sherpas, [prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostitution_in_Tibet) and gambling at the Tibet Base Camp, fraud related to the sale of oxygen bottles, and climbers collecting donations under the pretense of removing trash from the mountain.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309]&#x20;

The Chinese side of Everest in Tibet was described as "out of control" in 2007 after one Canadian had all his gear stolen and was abandoned by his Sherpa.[^cite_note-stolen-310] Another Sherpa helped the victim get off the mountain safely and gave him some spare gear. Other climbers have also reported missing oxygen bottles, which can be worth hundreds of dollars each. Hundreds of climbers pass by people's tents, making it hard to safeguard against theft.[^cite_note-stolen-310] In the late 2010s, the reports of theft of oxygen bottles from camps became more common.[^cite_note-311]&#x20;

### Timeline

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of climbing Mount Everest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_climbing_Mount_Everest).

See also: [List of Mount Everest records](39405618.md)



The Khumbu Icefall in 2005



The Western Cwm ("Coom"), with Everest on the left and [Lhotse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhotse) to the right

By the end of the 2010 climbing season, there had been 5,104 ascents to the summit by about 3,142 individuals.[^cite_note-8000ers-160] Some notable "firsts" by climbers include:&#x20;

- **1922**: First climb to 8,000 metres (26,247 ft), by [George Mallory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mallory), [Col. Felix Norton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Felix_Norton), and [Howard Somervell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Somervell)

- **1952**: First climb to [South Col](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Col) by [1952 Swiss Mount Everest expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Swiss_Mount_Everest_expedition)

- **1953**: First ascent, by [Tenzing Norgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzing_Norgay) and [Edmund Hillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Hillary) on [1953 British Mount Everest expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_British_Mount_Everest_expedition)

- **1960**: First reported ascent from the North Ridge by [Wang Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Fuzhou), [Gonpo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gongbu_\(mountaineer\)) and Qu Yinhua of China.

  [^cite_note-NorthRidge-16]

- **1975**: First female ascent, by [Junko Tabei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junko_Tabei) (16 May).

  [^cite_note-8000ers-160]

  [^cite_note-JapanTimes-312]

- **1975**: First female ascent from the North Ridge, by [Phanthog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanthog), deputy head of the second Chinese Everest expedition that sent nine climbers to the summit (27 May).

  [^cite_note-313]

  [^cite_note-314]

  [^cite_note-315]

- **1978**: First ascent without supplemental oxygen by [Reinhold Messner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinhold_Messner) and [Peter Habeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Habeler)

  [^cite_note-NOVA_fwo-316]

- **1978**: First solo ascent, by [Franz Oppurg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Oppurg)

  [^cite_note-317]

- **1980**: First winter ascent, by Polish National Expedition Winter 1979/1980 ([Leszek Cichy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leszek_Cichy) and [Krzysztof Wielicki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krzysztof_Wielicki)

  [^cite_note-318]

  [^cite_note-319]

  )

- **1980**: Second solo ascent, and the first without supplemental oxygen, by Reinhold Messner

  [^cite_note-NOVA_fwo-316]

- **1988**: First "cross-over" climb by Chinese, Japanese and Nepali teams which ascended the peak simultaneously from both the North and South sides of the mountain and descended down the other side.

  [^cite_note-JapanTimes-312]

  &#x20;The cross-over climb was also the first to be recorded on live broadcast television.

- **1988**: First descent by paraglider, by [Jean-Marc Boivin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marc_Boivin)

  [^cite_note-A_view_from_the_top_of_the_world-320]

- **1988**: First female ascent without supplemental oxygen by [Lydia Bradey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydia_Bradey)

  [^cite_note-321]

- **2000**: [Lhakpa Sherpa](50511396.md) becomes first Nepali woman to summit Everest and survive.

  [^cite_note-322]

- **2000**: First descent by ski by [Davo Karničar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davo_Karni%C4%8Dar)

  [^cite_note-323]

- **2001**: First descent by snowboard by [Marco Siffredi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marco_Siffredi)

  [^cite_note-Everest_News-324]

  [^cite_note-Smith-325]

- **2001**: First ascent by a blind climber, [Erik Weihenmayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_Weihenmayer)

  [^cite_note-326]

- **2025**: First descent by ski without supplemental oxygen by [Andrzej Bargiel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrzej_Bargiel)

  [^cite_note-\:1-327]

  [^cite_note-\:5-328]

## Aviation

### 1933: Flight over Everest

Main article: [Houston–Mount Everest flight expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston%E2%80%93Mount_Everest_flight_expedition)

[Lucy, Lady Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucy,_Lady_Houston), a British [millionaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millionaire) former [showgirl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Showgirl), funded the Houston Everest Flight of 1933. A formation of [airplanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airplane) led by the [Marquess of Clydesdale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Douglas-Hamilton,_14th_Duke_of_Hamilton) flew over the summit in an effort to photograph the unknown terrain.[^cite_note-flymicro-113]&#x20;

### 1988: First climb and glide

On 26 September 1988, having climbed the mountain via the Southeast Ridge, [Jean-Marc Boivin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marc_Boivin) made the first [paraglider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraglider) descent of Everest,[^cite_note-A_view_from_the_top_of_the_world-320] in the process creating the record for the fastest descent of the mountain and the highest paraglider flight. Boivin said: "I was tired when I reached the top because I had broken much of the trail, and to run at this altitude was quite hard."[^cite_note-Back-329]&#x20;

### 1991: Hot air balloon flyover

In 1991, four men in two balloons achieved the first [hot-air balloon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot-air_balloon) flight over Mount Everest.[^cite_note-balloon-330] In one balloon were Andy Elson and [Eric Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Jones_\(climber\)) (cameraman), and in the other balloon Chris Dewhirst and [Leo Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Dickinson) (cameraman).[^cite_note-elson-331] Dickinson went on to write a book about the adventure called *Ballooning Over Everest*.[^cite_note-elson-331] The hot-air balloons were modified to function at up to 12,000 m (40,000 ft) altitude.[^cite_note-elson-331] Reinhold Messner called one of Dickinson's panoramic views of Everest, captured on the now discontinued Kodak [Kodachrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kodachrome) film, the "best snap on Earth", according to UK newspaper *[The Telegraph](23797577.md)*.[^cite_note-332] Dewhirst has offered to take passengers on a repeat of this feat for US\$2.6 million per passenger.[^cite_note-balloon-330]&#x20;

### 2005: Pilot summits with helicopter



Photo of a Eurocopter AS350 B3 "Squirrel"

In May 2005, pilot [Didier Delsalle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didier_Delsalle) of France landed a [Eurocopter AS350](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocopter_AS350) B3 helicopter on the summit of Mount Everest.[^cite_note-eurocopter-333] He needed to land for two minutes to set the [Fédération Aéronautique Internationale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9d%C3%A9ration_A%C3%A9ronautique_Internationale) (FAI) official record, but he stayed for about four minutes, twice.[^cite_note-eurocopter-333] In this type of landing the rotors stay engaged, which avoids relying on the snow to fully support the aircraft. The flight set [rotorcraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotorcraft) world [records](39405618.md), for highest of both landing and take-off.[^cite_note-fai-334]&#x20;

Some press reports suggested that the report of the summit landing was a misunderstanding of a South Col landing, but he had also landed on South Col two days earlier,[^cite_note-335] with this landing and the Everest records confirmed by the FAI.[^cite_note-fai-334] Delsalle also rescued two Japanese climbers at 4,880 m (16,000 ft) while he was there. One climber noted that the new record meant a better chance of rescue.[^cite_note-eurocopter-333]&#x20;

### 2011: Paraglide off summit

On 21 May 2011, Nepalis Lakpa Tsheri Sherpa and Sano Babu Sunuwar paraglided from Everest's summit to [Namche Bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namche_Bazaar) in 42 minutes.[^cite_note-abcnews1-285][^cite_note-336] After the flight they hiked, biked, and [kayaked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kayaking) to the Indian Ocean, reaching the Bay of Bengal by 27 June 2011, thereby becoming the first people to complete a continuous summit-to-sea descent from Everest.[^cite_note-nationalgeographic1-337] They accomplished the ground-breaking feat despite Babu having never previously climbed, and Lakpa having never kayaked and not even knowing how to swim.[^cite_note-nationalgeographic1-337] The duo subsequently won National Geographic Adventurers of the Year for 2012 for their exploits.[^cite_note-nationalgeographic1-337] In 2013 footage of the flight was shown on the television news program *[Nightline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightline)*.[^cite_note-338]&#x20;

### 2014: Helicopter-assisted ascent

In 2014, a team financed and led by mountaineer [Wang Jing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Jing_\(mountaineer\)) used a helicopter to fly from South Base Camp to Camp II to avoid the Khumbu Icefall, and thence climbed to the Everest summit.[^cite_note-nationalgeographic2-339] This climb immediately sparked outrage and controversy in much of the mountaineering world over the legitimacy and propriety of her climb.[^cite_note-news.nationalgeographic.com-340][^cite_note-341] Nepal ended up investigating Wang, who initially denied the claim that she had flown to Camp II, admitting only that some support crew were flown to that higher camp, over the Khumbu Icefall.[^cite_note-342] In August 2014, however, she stated that she had flown to Camp II because the icefall was impassable. "If you don't fly to Camp II, you just go home", she said in an interview. In that same interview, she also insisted that she had never tried to hide this fact.[^cite_note-news.nationalgeographic.com-340]&#x20;

Her team had had to use the south side because the Chinese had denied them a permit to climb. Ultimately, the Chinese refusal may have been beneficial to Nepal's interests, allowing the government to showcase improved local hospitals and providing the opportunity for a new hybrid aviation/mountaineering style, triggering discussions about helicopter use in the mountaineering world.[^cite_note-news.nationalgeographic.com-340] National Geographic noted that a village festooned Wang with honours after she donated US\$30,000 to the town's hospital. Wang won the [International Mountaineer of the Year Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=International_Mountaineer_of_the_Year_Award\&action=edit\&redlink=1) from the Nepal government in June 2014.[^cite_note-nationalgeographic2-339]&#x20;

### 2016: Helicopter business increases

In 2016 the increased use of helicopters was noted for increased efficiency and for hauling material over the deadly Khumbu icefall.[^cite_note-blogs.dw\.com-343] In particular it was noted that flights saved icefall porters 80 trips but still increased commercial activity at Everest.[^cite_note-blogs.dw\.com-343] After many Nepalis died in the icefall in 2014, the government had wanted helicopters to handle more transportation to Camp 1 but this was not possible because of the 2015 earthquake closing the mountain, so this was then implemented in 2016 (helicopters did prove instrumental in rescuing many people in 2015 though).[^cite_note-blogs.dw\.com-343] That summer Bell tested the [412EPI](3101264.md#Variants), which conducted a series of tests including hovering at 5,500 m (18,000 ft) and flying as high as 6,100 m (20,000 ft) altitude near Mount Everest.[^cite_note-344]&#x20;

## Extreme sports

Mount Everest has been host to other winter sports and adventuring besides mountaineering, including snowboarding, skiing, paragliding, and BASE jumping.&#x20;

[Yuichiro Miura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuichiro_Miura) became the first man to ski down Everest in the 1970s. He descended nearly 1,300 vertical metres (4,200 ft) from the South Col before falling with extreme injuries.[^cite_note-japtimes-103] Stefan Gatt and Marco Siffredi snowboarded Mount Everest in 2001.[^cite_note-TransWorld_SNOWboarding-345] Other Everest skiers include [Davo Karničar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davo_Karni%C4%8Dar) of Slovenia, who completed a top to South Base Camp descent in 2000, [Hans Kammerlander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Kammerlander) of Italy in 1996 on the north side, and Kit DesLauriers of the United States in 2006.[^cite_note-346] Marco Siffredi died in 2002 on his second snow-boarding expedition.[^cite_note-TransWorld_SNOWboarding-345] In 2025, [Andrzej Bargiel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrzej_Bargiel) completed the first descent by ski without supplemental oxygen.[^cite_note-\:1-327][^cite_note-\:5-328]&#x20;

Various types of gliding descents have slowly become more popular, and are noted for their rapid descents to lower camps. In 1986 [Steve McKinney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_McKinney_\(skier\)) led an expedition to Mount Everest.[^cite_note-347] Frenchman [Jean-Marc Boivin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marc_Boivin) made the first [paraglider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraglider) descent of Everest in September 1988, descending in minutes from the Southeast Ridge to a lower camp.[^cite_note-A_view_from_the_top_of_the_world-320] In 2011, Nepalis Sano Babu Sunuwar and Lakpa Tsheri Sherpa made a gliding descent from the Everest summit down 5,000 metres (16,400 ft) in 45 minutes.[^cite_note-348]&#x20;

## Religious significance



The [Rongbuk Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rongbuk_Monastery), with Mount Everest in the background

The southern part of Mount Everest is regarded as one of several "hidden valleys" of refuge designated by [Padmasambhava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padmasambhava), a ninth-century "[lotus-born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lotus_birth)" Buddhist saint.[^cite_note-natgeo1-349]&#x20;

Near the base of the north side of Everest lies [Rongbuk Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rongbuk_Monastery), which has been called the "sacred threshold to Mount Everest, with the most dramatic views of the world."[^cite_note-350] For [Sherpas](224737.md) living on the slopes of Everest in the [Khumbu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu) region of Nepal, Rongbuk Monastery is an important pilgrimage site, accessed in a few days of travel across the Himalayas through [Nangpa La](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nangpa_La).[^cite_note-Ullman-119]&#x20;

[Miyolangsangma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miyolangsangma), a [Tibetan](31516.md) [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist) "*Goddess of Inexhaustible Giving*", is believed to have lived at the top of Mount Everest. According to Sherpa Buddhist monks, Mount Everest is Miyolangsangma's palace and playground, and all climbers are only partially welcome guests, having arrived without invitation.[^cite_note-natgeo1-349]&#x20;

The [Sherpa people](224737.md) also believe that Mount Everest and its flanks are blessed with spiritual energy, and one should show reverence when passing through this sacred landscape. Here, the [karmic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma) effects of one's actions are magnified, and impure thoughts are best avoided.[^cite_note-natgeo1-349]&#x20;

## Waste management

Further information: [Impacts of tourism § Mount Everest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impacts_of_tourism#Mount_Everest)

In 2015, the president of the Nepal Mountaineering Association warned that pollution, especially human waste, has reached critical levels. As much as 12,000 kg (26,500 lb) of human excrement each season is left behind on the mountain.[^cite_note-351] Human waste is strewn across the verges of the route to the summit, making the four sleeping areas on the route up Everest's south side minefields of human excrement. Climbers above Base Camp—for the 62-year history of climbing on the mountain—have most commonly either buried their excrement in holes they dug by hand in the snow, or slung it into crevasses, or simply defecated wherever convenient, often within metres of their tents. The only place where climbers can defecate without worrying about contaminating the mountain is Base Camp. At approximately 5,500 m (18,000 ft), Base Camp sees the most activity of all camps on Everest because climbers acclimate and rest there. In the late-1990s, expeditions began using toilets that they fashioned from blue plastic 190-litre (50-US-gallon) barrels fitted with a toilet seat and enclosed.[^cite_note-352]&#x20;

The problem of human waste is compounded by the presence of more anodyne waste: spent oxygen tanks, abandoned tents, empty cans and bottles. The Nepali government now requires each climber to pack out eight kilograms of waste when descending the mountain.[^cite_note-353]&#x20;

In February 2019, due to the mounting waste problem, China closed the base camp on its side of Everest to visitors without climbing permits. Tourists are allowed to go as far as the [Rongbuk Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rongbuk_Monastery).[^cite_note-bbc_2019-206]&#x20;

In April 2019, the [Solukhumbu district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solukhumbu_District)'s [Khumbu Pasanglhamu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumbu_Pasanglhamu) Rural Municipality launched a campaign to collect nearly 10,000 kg (20,000 lb) of garbage from Everest.[^cite_note-354] Five years later, 2024, waste removal is receiving continuing attention.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357]&#x20;

For more details, see *[1924 British Mount Everest expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_British_Mount_Everest_expedition)*.

[^cite_note-base-53]: &#x20;The "base" of a mountain is a problematic notion in general with no universally accepted definition. However, for a peak rising out of relatively flat terrain, such as Mauna Kea or Denali, an "approximate" height above "base" can be calculated. Everest is more complicated since it only rises above relatively flat terrain on its north (Tibetan Plateau) side. Hence the concept of "base" has even less meaning for Everest than for Mauna Kea or Denali, and the range of numbers for "height above base" is wider. In general, comparisons based on "height above base" are somewhat suspect.

Moore explains that these jet streaks can drag a huge draught of air up the side of the mountain, lowering the air pressure. He calculates that this typically reduces the partial pressure of oxygen in the air by about 6%, which translates to a 14% reduction in oxygen uptake for the climbers. Air at that altitude already contains only one third as much oxygen as sea-level air.
